CHAPTER 3: PREVENTION & MANAGEMENT OF COPD
KEY POINTS:
• All individuals who smoke should be strongly encouraged and supported to quit. Nicotine replacement and pharmacotherapy reliably increase long-term smoking abstinence rates.
Legislative smoking bans and counseling, delivered by healthcare professionals, improve quit rates.
There is no evidence to support the effectiveness and safety of e-cigarettes as a smoking cessation aid at present.
• The main treatment goals are to reduce symptoms and future risk of exacerbations. The management strategy of stable COPD should be predominantly based on the assessment of symptoms and the history of exacerbations.
• Pharmacotherapy can reduce COPD symptoms, reduce the frequencUy and severity of
B exacerbations, and improve health status and exercise tolerance. Data suggIest beneficial effects on
R rates of lung function decline and mortality. T
• Each pharmacological treatment regimen should be individualized and guided by the severity of
R symptoms, risk of exacerbations, side-effects, comorbidities, drug availability and cost, and the
O patient’s response, preference, and ability to use various dru g delivery devices.
• Inhaler technique needs to be assessed regularly.
• COVID-19 vaccines are highly effective against SOARS-CoV-2 infection and people with COPD should
N have the COVID-19 vaccination in line with national recommendations.
• Influenza vaccination and pneumococca l vaccination decrease the incidence of lower respiratory
- tract infections. L
• The CDC recommends: the TdaEp vaccination (dTaP/dTPa; pertussis, tetanus and diptheria) for COPD
T patients who were not vaccinated in adolescence; routine use of shingles vaccine in all COPD
A patients; the new respiraMtory syncytial virus (RSV) vaccine for individuals over 60 years and/or with chronic heart or lung Tdisease.
• Pulmonary rehaIbilitation with its core components, including exercise training combined with
R disease-specYific education, improves exercise capacity, symptoms, and quality of life across all
P grades oOf COPD severity.
• In patients with severe resting chronic hypoxemia (PaO ≤ 55 mmHg or < 60 mmHg if there is cor pulmonale or secondary polycythemia), long-term oxygen therapy improves survival.
• In patients with stable COPD and resting or exercise-induced moderate desaturation, long-term oxygen treatment should not be prescribed routinely. However, individual patient factors must be considered when evaluating the patient’s need for supplemental oxygen.
• In patients with severe chronic hypercapnia and a history of hospitalization for acute respiratory failure, long-term non-invasive ventilation may decrease mortality and prevent re-hospitalization.
• In select patients with advanced emphysema refractory to optimized medical care, surgical or bronchoscopic interventional treatments may be beneficial.
• Palliative approaches are effective in controlling symptoms in advanced COPD.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 45
INTRODUCTION
The aim of COPD management is to reduce symptoms and future risk (Figure 3.1). COPD patients should have an assessment of the severity of their airflow obstruction, symptoms, history of exacerbations, exposure to risk factors and comorbidities (see Chapter 2) to guide management.
Pharmacological and non-pharmacological therapy s hould be adjusted as necessary (see below) and further reviews
 L undertaken (Figure 3.2). This chapter containsA recommendations on how to manage patients with COPD in clinical
I practice and summarizes the evidence aboutR the effectiveness and safety of maintenance and prevention strategies
E in COPD on which the recommendations Tare based.
IDENTIFY AND REDUCE EXPOSURE TO RISK FACTORS
Identification and reducPtion of exposure to risk factors is important not only for the primary prevention of COPD but
O also as part of the mCanagement of a COPD patient. Cigarette smoking is the most commonly encountered and easily identifiable risk factor for COPD, and smoking cessation should be continually encouraged for all individuals who smoke. Reduction of total personal exposure to occupational dusts, fumes, and gases, and to household and outdoor air pollutants, should also be addressed (Figure 3.3).
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 46
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 47
Smoking cessation
Smoking cessation is a key intervention for all COPD patients who continue to smoke. Healthcare providers are pivotal in delivering smoking cessation messages and interventions and should encourage patients to quit at every available opportunity (Figure 3.4).
A significant proportion of people with COPD continue to smoke despite knowing they have the disease (approximately
40% of those with COPD are current smokers), and continued smoking has a negative impact on prognosis and progression of the disease.(585) Smoking cessation has the greatest capacity to influence the natural history of COPD, it also improves daily symptoms,(586) and decreases the frequency of exacerbations.(587)
For smokers with COPD, the quitting may be more challenging than for smokers without COPD due to greater nicotine dependence, lower self-efficacy and lower self-esteem.(588-590) In addition, it has been reported that depression is more
E common in smokers with COPD(591) and this could contribute to failed attempts to quit.(591,5T92) Despite these adverse
U conditions, if effective time and resources are dedicated to smoking cessation, long-term quit rates of 14% to 27%
I have been reported.(592) R
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 48
As for all smokers, smoking cessation treatment for people with COPD should be adapted to the individual’s needs and according to the level of tobacco dependence. There is evidence that a combination of counseling and pharmacotherapy is the most effective smoking cessation treatment for people with COPD.(592-594) The complexity of the smoking cessation process is largely determined by nicotine addiction. An accurate assessment of nicotine dependence should therefore be carried out for all patients. Some indicators of high nicotine dependence are: smoking within 30 min of waking up, smoking at night, consuming ≥ 20 cigarettes per day, a score of 7 to 10 on the Fagerström scale or 5 to 6 on the Heaviness of Smoking Index.(595,596)
In addition to individual approaches to smoking cessation, legislative smoking bans are effective in increasing quit rates and reducing harm from second-hand smoke exposure.(597)
Advice and counseling interventions
A five-step program for intervention (Figure 3.4)(598-600) provides a helpful strategic framework to guide healthcare providers interested in helping their patients stop smoking.(598,600,601) When possible, the patienEt should be referred to
T a comprehensive smoking cessation program that incorporates behavioral techniques that eUnhance patient motivation
B and confidence, patient education, and pharmacological and non-pharmacological inteIrventions. Recommendations
R for treating tobacco use and dependence are summarized in Figure 3.5.(598) BecauseT tobacco dependence is a chronic
S disease,(598,600) clinicians should recognize that relapse is common and reflects tDheI chronic nature of dependence and addiction, and does not represent failure on the part of the patient or the cliRnician.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 49
Counseling delivered by physicians and other health professionals significantly increases quit rates over self-initiated strategies.(602) Even brief (3-minute) periods of counseling urging a smoker to quit improve smoking cessation rates.(602)
In patients with COPD a network meta-analysis showed a trend for smoking cessation counseling alone to be superior than usual care.(603) Another study reported a continuous one-year abstinence rate of 1.4% for usual care, 2.6% for minimal counseling (< 90 min), 6% for intensive counseling (≥ 90 min), and 12.3% for intensive counseling plus pharmacotherapy.(593) However, controversy remains as to whether more intensive individual counseling is more effective when combined with pharmacotherapy.(603)
Household and outdoor air pollution
Reducing exposure to household and outdoor air pollution requires a combination of public policy, local and national resources, cultural changes, and protective steps taken by individual patients. Reduction of exEposure to smoke from
T biomass fuel is a crucial goal to reduce the prevalence of COPD worldwide. Efficient ventilation, non-polluting cooking
U stoves and similar interventions are feasible and should be recommended.(8,9) B
Occupational exposures
There are no studies that demonstrate whether interventions that rPeduce occupational exposures also reduce the
O burden of COPD, but it seems logical to advise patients to avoid ongoing exposures to potential irritants e.g., dusts,
C fumes and gases, if possible. T
Climate change & COPD D
Climate change has increased the frequency,R inItensity and geographic distribution of extreme weather events such as
E heatwaves, severe winter conditions, floods, droughts, hurricanes, thunderstorms, wildfires and dust storms. Excess
T deaths have been reported during heat A waves in all regions of the world,(604-610) and during periods of cold weather.(611 M
614) Epidemiological studies confirm the association between exposure to extreme, but not moderate, heat and
H mortality,(615-617) with the mortality impact of extreme high temperatures increasing with the length of the heat
G episode.(618) Temperature thrIesholds for given health impacts are unique to particular locations, with greater effects
Y of heat in large cities and colder regions where extreme temperatures were not previously common, and greater
P effects of falls in temOperature in warmer regions that were unused to cold.(611,617) The effects of heat also vary with
C levels of atmospheric humidity and air pollution.(605,619,620)
Population studies have consistently found that people with COPD are at increased risk of death as a consequence of exposure to both heat and cold,(621-623) with cold leading to a greater risk than heat.(624,625) As well as the effects on mortality, higher outdoor temperatures are associated with an increased risk of hospitalization for COPD(623,626-628) and lower outdoor temperatures are associated with an increased risk of exacerbations.(629)
Most studies have examined the effect of outdoor air temperature on mortality and morbidity in COPD; however, many patients, particularly those with more severe disease, spend much of their time indoors. Indoor temperature and humidity vary significantly, despite similar outdoor conditions and are influenced by the building type, insulation, presence of heating, sun shades and ventilation, socioeconomic factors including the ability to afford heating, and behavioral factors such as cooking and bathing.(630-632) Indoor temperatures are often higher than outdoor including during heatwaves,(633,634) and high indoor humidity can exacerbate the adverse effects of heat by impeding natural
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 50 evaporative cooling. Lower indoor temperatures during cold periods are found in older properties, in those lacking heating systems and those that are expensive to heat.(635) Even if heating is available, COPD patients may not heat their bedrooms at night and may keep windows open overnight even in winter.(636,637)
Studies of the relationship between temperature, symptoms and lung function in COPD at an individual patient level have shown higher outdoor temperature is associated with increased dyspnea(638) particularly on days that patients go outside.(639) Higher indoor temperatures are associated with increased symptoms and SABA use(639) and colder indoor and outdoor temperatures are also associated with increased cough and sputum, increased SABA use and a fall in FEV1.(636,638-640) Over an autumn, winter and spring period (with average minimum outdoor temperature of 2.9°C and maximum of 10.1°C) health status was better in patients with at least 9 hours of indoor temperature ≥ 21°C.(641)
Weather also has a significant impact on air quality. Ozone levels strongly correlate with temperature, as its generation depends on high temperatures and sunlight,(642) and the generation and dispersion of air pollutants may be influenced by local patterns of wind, solar radiation and precipitation, especially in urban areas.(643) Some studies have shown
E synergistic effects between heat and air pollution exposure on all-cause mortality, and hospiTtal admissions in general
U populations;(644-646) however, there are also studies showing no interaction.(647,648) BA synergistic effect of low
R temperatures and ozone concentrations on the general population has also been found in some countries (e.g., China
T and Hong Kong(649,650)) but not in others(621,644) Although not widely studied, there i S s conflicting evidence regarding the
D interactive effects of outdoor heat and pollution on respiratory outcomes.(646,65 1,652) Several studies have looked at the
R interactive effects of pollution and temperature in people with COPD. O There appears to be a greater effect of pollutants on COPD hospital admissions and emergency visits at low temYperatures or during winter.(653-656) Outdoor air
P pollutants, including PM2.5, NO , and ozone do not appear to Oworsen the effect of outdoor temperature on
C symptoms,(657) but the detrimental effect of higher indoor temp eratures on symptoms appears to be potentiated by
T higher indoor concentrations of PM2.5 and NO .(657) ThOe conflicting findings regarding interactions between
N temperature and pollutants on symptoms and exacerbati ons in people with COPD may, in part, reflect differences in
O behaviors that affect exposure, such as avoiding goingD out, opening windows and using air conditioning.
The mechanisms by which heat exposure and cold adversely impact COPD are not well understood. However, on the
R basis of the evidence showing a relationshipE between high and low temperatures and morbidity and mortality, patients
T with COPD living in temperate and colder climates should, in line with WHO recommendations,(658) ensure they keep
M bedroom temperatures above 18°C during cold weather. During heatwaves and periods of high temperature patients
T should ensure they keep adequaHtely hydrated, keep out of the heat and try to keep living spaces < 32°C and sleeping
G spaces < 24°C as recommended by WHO.(659) Prior identification and management of cardiovascular comorbidities is
R also important to reduce adverse outcomes.
There are significanCt differences in the carbon footprint of inhaler devices reflecting whether they contain a propellant gas, what they are made from, how they are manufactured and transported, and whether they can be reused or recycled. Selection and correct use and disposal of inhalers by patients can have important implications for global warming and climate change. Similarly, models of care should consider the environmental impact of healthcare facilities and the need for patients to travel.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 51
Vaccinations
People with COPD should receive all recommended vaccinations in line with the relevant local guidelines (Figure 3.6).
Influenza vaccine
Influenza vaccination can reduce serious illness (such as lower respiratory tract infections requiring hospitalization)(660) stroke,(661) and death in people with COPD.(662-665) Only a few studies have evaluated exacerbations and they have shown significant reduction in the total number of exacerbations per vaccinated subject compared with those who received placebo.(662) Vaccines containing either killed or live inactivated viruses are recommended(666) as they are more effective in elderly people with COPD.(667) The CDC recommends high-dose inactivated (HD-IIV3) and adjuvanted inactivated (aIIV3) influenza vaccines as acceptable options for influenza vaccination in older adults.(668) Findings from a population-based study suggested that people with COPD, particularly the elderly, had decreased risk of ischemic heart disease when they were vaccinated with influenza vaccine over many years.(669) OccurrenEce of adverse reactions
T is generally mild and transient. U
Pneumococcal vaccines
Pneumococcal vaccinations, pneumococcal conjugated vaccine (PCV20 or PCV1D5) and pneumococcal polysaccharide
R vaccine (PPSV23), are approved for adults aged ≥ 65 years. They are also approved for adults aged 19-64 years if they
O have an underlying medical condition such as chronic lung disease (in cluding COPD, emphysema, and asthma),
Y cigarette smoking, solid organ transplant etc. The FDA recently approv P ed PCV21(670) which covers 11 unique serotypes
O not included in PCV20; many cases of adult disease are causedC by subtypes not covered by other FDA-approved
T pneumococcal vaccines. Pneumococcal vaccination is universally recommended for adults in these age groups, if they
O have never received a pneumococcal conjugate vaccine pNreviously, or if their previous pneumococcal vaccination
O history is unknown. The current recommendation is PCV15 followed by PPSV23 or one dose PCV20,(671) or one dose
PCV21(670) in adults who have indication for the vacci ne (Figure 3.6).(671)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 52
Specific data on the effects of PPSV and PCV in people with COPD are limited.(672) A systematic review of injectable vaccines in COPD patients identified twelve randomized studies for inclusion and observed injectable polyvalent pneumococcal vaccination provides significant protection against community-acquired pneumonia, although no evidence indicates that vaccination reduced the risk of confirmed pneumococcal pneumonia, which was a relatively rare event. Vaccination reduced the likelihood of a COPD exacerbation, and moderate-quality evidence suggests the benefits of pneumococcal vaccination in COPD patients. Evidence was insufficient for comparison of different pneumococcal vaccine types.(673) PPSV23 has been shown to reduce the incidence of community-acquired pneumonia in COPD patients < 65 years, with an FEV1 < 40% predicted, or comorbidities (especially cardiac comorbidities).(674) The
PCV13 has been shown to exhibit at least the same or greater immunogenicity than the PPSV23 up to two years after vaccination in COPD patients.(675) In a large RCT PCV13 demonstrated significant efficacy for the prevention of vaccine- type community-acquired pneumonia (45.6%) and vaccine-type invasive pneumococcal disease (75%) among adults ≥
65 years and the efficacy persisted for at least 4 years.(676) PCV20 covers up to 58% of those strains, while PCV21 covers up to 84% of strains responsible for invasive disease in adults.(677)
A study compared the effectiveness of PPSV23 and PCV13 in COPD patients over a 5-yearT follow-up cohort study.
Although both vaccines have comparable clinical effects during the first year after Bvaccination, PCV13 showed
R persistent clinical effectiveness during the 5-year follow-up period. Pneumonia by year 5 after vaccination was
S registered in 47% of patients in the PPSV23 group, versus 3.3% of patients in the PCV13 group (p < 0.001). Similar
D effects were shown in the reduction of COPD exacerbations.(678)
Pneumococcal vaccine can be co-administered with influenza vaccYine in an adult immunization program, as
P concomitant administration (PCV15 or PPSV23 and QIV [Fluarix]O, PCV20 and adjuvanted QIV [Fluad]) has been
C demonstrated to be immunogenic and safe.(679)
Respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) vaccine
The US Centers for Disease Control (CDC) estima tes that every year, respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) causes
- approximately 60,000–160,000 hospitalizations aLnd 6,000–10,000 deaths among older adults in the USA.(680,681) RSV
A infection in adults may have a significant impRacIt not only in the respiratory system but also on other organs. In a cross E sectional study over 5 RSV seasons, nearly one-quarter of hospitalized adults aged ≥ 60 years with RSV infection
A experienced an acute cardiac event (most frequently acute heart failure), including 1 in 12 adults (8.5%) with no
M documented underlying cardiovasc ular disease.(682) In a report from the UK, RSV was associated with 8.7% of
H outpatient managed COPD exacerbations.(683)
The Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices’ (ACIP) and the European Commission recommend use of RSV
P bivalent prefusion F pOrotein-based vaccine,(684) and prefusion F protein vaccine(685) for all individuals aged ≥ 75 years
C and for those ≥ 60 years at high risk of severe RSV illness due to chronic heart or lung disease, immune compromise, or living in a nursing home or long-term care facility.(686) Recent analysis of follow-up demonstrated that one dose of adjuvanted prefusion F protein-based vaccine was efficacious in preventing RSV-related disease in participants aged ≥
60 years during 2 full RSV seasons.(687) In another study of 2,987 adults ≥ 60 years, RSV vaccination reduced the likelihood of RSV-associated hospitalization. Moreover, the study demonstrated protection in a population at high risk of severe RSV disease, including adults aged ≥ 75 years and immunocompromised individuals.(688) The safety of RSV vaccinations was demonstrated in a recent CDC publication.(689)
Other vaccines
COPD patients are at increased risk of pertussis (whooping cough).(690) As such, in adults with COPD the CDC recommends the Tdap vaccination (also called dTaP/dTPa) to protect against pertussis, in addition to tetanus and diphtheria, in those who were not vaccinated in adolescence and also the routine use of shingles vaccine.(691,692)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 53
People with COPD should have the COVID-19 vaccinations in line with national recommendations.(693) COVID-19 vaccines are highly effective against SARS-CoV-2 infection requiring hospitalization, ICU admission, or an emergency department or urgent care clinic visit, including those with chronic respiratory disease.(693,694)
PHARMACOLOGICAL TREATMENT OF STABLE COPD
We propose a tailored approach to initiate treatment based on the level of symptoms and risk for exacerbations.
Treatment can be escalated/de-escalated based on the presence of the predominant symptoms (treatable traits) of breathlessness and exercise limitation, and the continued occurrence of exacerbations whilst on maintenance therapy.
The basis for these recommendations was partly derived from evidence generated from randomized controlled trials.
However, as these recommendations are intended to support clinician decision-making, they also incorporate expert advice based on clinical experience.
Initial pharmacotherapy should be based on the patient’s GOLD group (Figure 3.7). Patients should be offered
U guidance and follow up on self-management of breathlessness, and stress management, Band be given a written action
R plan (Figure 3.13). Comorbidities should be managed as per specific guidelines, irreTspective of the presence of COPD
(Figure 3.2).
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 54
Patients should be reviewed after a suitable interval (shorter in more severe patients and longer in less severe patients) and their current level of symptoms (using either the CAT or mMRC scores) and exacerbation frequency assessed. The effect of treatment and possible adverse effects should be evaluated, and comorbidities reassessed.
Inhaler technique, adherence to prescribed therapy (both pharmacological and non-pharmacological), smoking status and continued exposure to risk factors should be regularly checked. Physical activity should be encouraged and referral for pulmonary rehabilitation considered in severe patients. The need for oxygen therapy, non-invasive ventilatory support, lung volume reduction and palliative approaches should be considered individually, and the action plan should be updated accordingly. Spirometry should be repeated at least annually. If the patient is already receiving bronchodilator treatment, the latter should not be interrupted for performing spirometry.
We no longer refer to asthma and COPD overlap (ACO), instead we emphasize that asthma and COPD are different disorders, although they may share some common treatable traits and clinical features (e.g., eosinophilia, some degree of reversibility). Asthma and COPD may coexist in an individual patient. If a concurrent diagnosis of asthma is
E suspected, pharmacotherapy should primarily follow asthma guidelines. T

► If a LABA+LAMA combination is not considered appropriate, there is no evidence to recommend one class of long- acting bronchodilators over another (LABA or LAMA) for initial relief of symptoms in this group of patients. In the individual patient, the choice should depend on the patient’s perception of symptom relief.
► Group B patients are likely to have comorbidities that may add to their symptomatology and impact their prognosis,

Managing inhaled therapy
Most of the drugs used to treat COPD are inhaled. Thus, appropriate use of inhaler devices is crucial to optimize the benefit-risk ratio of inhaled therapy. Achieving this goal requires choosing the appropriate device, providing education, checking inhaler use regularly and, whenever necessary, adapting education and device (Figure 3.10).
 There are currently at least 33 different inhaled thLe rapies containing different bronchodilators (both short- and long A acting) and inhaled corticosteroids (ICS) alone oIr in combinations (Figure 3.18). In addition, at least 22 different inhaler
R devices are available,(711) including nebulizEers, metered-dose inhalers (MDIs) used with or without valved holding
T chamber (VHC)/spacers, breath-actuateAd MDIs (BAIs), soft mist inhalers (SMIs) and dry powder inhalers (DPIs)(712) In
M multi-dose DPIs, the powder is conta ined in a reservoir or in individual blisters.(712) More information about inhalation
T devices is available on the AsthmHa + Lung UK website.(713)
Devices differ in their sizeY and portability. They also differ in the number of steps required to prepare them,(714) in the
P force needed to loadO or actuate them,(715) in the time taken to deliver the drug, and in the need for cleaning and
C maintenance, as well as in the inspiratory manoeuvre required to use them effectively.(712) Increased steps reduces the ease of use and likelihood that patients use the inhaler correctly.(716) There may also be quite significant differences in the carbon footprint of devices reflecting whether or not they contain a propellant gas, what they are made from, how they are manufactured and transported, and whether they can be reused or recycled.(717) The proper use of an inhaler has a positive environmental impact through the reduction of exacerbations and their CO footprint (especially when hospitalization is required).(717) Smart inhalers incorporate sensors that detect the date and time of use, and for some inspiratory flow and inspired volume. These allow the identification of problems and feedback in real time(718) and can provide objective data on adherence and technique.(719,720)
Particles > 5 microns (µm) are most likely to be deposited in the oropharynx. For drug delivery to the lower respiratory tract and lungs, particle size (mass-median aerodynamic diameter) can be fine (2-5 µm) or extra-fine (< 2 µm), which influences the total respirable fraction (particles < 5 µm) and the amount and site of drug deposition (more peripheral deposition with extra-fine particles).(712) Inspiratory flow, flow acceleration, and inhaled volume are important factors
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 60 for patients to successfully inhale drug particles from handheld devices into the lower respiratory tract.(712,721) MDIs and SMIs require a slow and deep inspiration while DPIs require forceful inspiration. Each DPI has a unique internal resistance and patients must create turbulent energy within the device during inhalation to disaggregate the powder into fine particles. Prescribers should check visually that the patient can inhale forcefully through the device and, if there is doubt, either check the inspiratory flow objectively(722,723) or switch to an MDI+/-spacer/VHC or SMI depending on drug availability and patient’s characteristics. Suboptimal peak inspiratory flow and inhalation technique errors were associated with higher COPD-related healthcare utilization and costs in patients on DPI maintenance therapy.(724)
Randomized controlled trials have not identified superiority of one device/formulation and there is no evidence for superiority of nebulized therapy over hand-held devices in patients who are able to use these devices properly.(712)
However, patients included in these trials are usually those who master inhalation technique and receive proper education and follow-up regarding this issue, and therefore may not be reflective of normal clinical practice. Fixed- dose triple inhaled combination therapy in one inhaler may help improve health status compared to treatment using multiple inhalers.(725)
Ability to use delivery system correctly B
Specific instructions are available for each type of device.(712,713) On average more thTan two thirds of patients make at
S least one error in using an inhalational device.(726-729) Observational studies in theIse patients show that, although the
D type and frequency of inhalation errors vary between devices depending on t heir characteristics, there is no device
R obviating the need to explain, demonstrate and regularly check inhalation tOechnique.(730-736) The main errors in delivery
Y device use relate to problems with inspiratory flow, inhalation duration, coordination, dose preparation, exhalation
P maneuver prior to inhalation and breath-holding following dose inh O alation.(737)
Patients’ ability to use inhalers correctly is affected by their cognitive ability, manual dexterity and coordination skills,
N the inspiratory flow that they can achieve, the use of Odif ferent types of device, and previous education on inhaler technique.(727,738) Poor inhaler technique and errors uDsing devices are more common with advancing age,(739) but this
- is likely to be mainly due to cofounders such asL cognitive impairment or reduced manual dexterity.(740,741) pMDIs
A require sufficient hand strength to actuate the Iinhaler, and although BAIs are triggered by inhalation they still require
R priming which needs a degree of strength.E(715) Patients with poor dexterity may struggle to load a DPI, particularly if
T capsules require extraction from foil, inAsertion into the device or puncturing prior to administration.(715) Tremor may
M result in shaking of the device and loss of the dose.(742)
If there is any doubt that the patient will not be able to use a pMDI correctly they should be prescribed a VHC/spacer;
R however, these are not aY panacea and there is evidence that fewer older patients using a pMDI and VHC find them
P easy to use comparedO to a pMDI alone.(743) Currently available VHCs range in volume from < 50 to 750 mL(744) but VHC
C with volumes from 150 to 250 mL have been shown to be as effective as those with larger volumes(745) and are more portable. As well as reducing difficulties caused by poor co-ordination and inspiratory maneuvers with pMDIs, VHCs increase pulmonary and reduce oropharyngeal deposition, which is particularly important to minimize the risk of oropharyngeal candidiasis with corticosteroid containing pMDIs.(712)
Leaflets included in device packages are insufficient to provide proper education of patients regarding inhaler use.
Other strategies and tools including physical training and use of video or web-based education have proven effective to improve inhaler technique in some but not all patients on the short-term, but effects appear to wane over time.(716)
Using the “teach-back” approach (patients being asked to show how the device has to be used) appears to be particularly effective.(746) Pharmacist-, physician-, physiotherapist- and nurse-led interventions(747) as well as lay health coaching(748) can improve inhalation technique and adherence in COPD patients. As in asthma, digital inhalers could contribute to improved adherence and inhaler technique in patients with COPD.(749)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 61
Choice of inhaler device
If a patient is currently taking inhaled therapy and able to use their current device correctly, new therapy is best prescribed in the same device. If a new device is required, either because the patient is not using their current device correctly or the drug is not available in the same device, a systematic iterative process should be used to select a delivery system and ensure the patient can use it.
The choice of an inhaler device depends on drug availability, characteristics of the device, the patient’s abilities and preferences, and the knowledge of healthcare professionals caring for the patient about devices and their correct usage. The final choice should be made jointly by the prescriber and the patient using a shared decision-making approach. Figure 3.11 summarises the main principles that should be considered to guide the individualized selection of the appropriate device for a given patient.
Appropriate education must be provided by health care professionals, including physical, video- or be-based demonstration of the proper technique and live verification that the patient masters this technique. It is crucial to check regularly (ideally, at each visit) that patients continue to use their device correctly. Lack of placebo devices within clinical areas is often a limitation and barrier to providing quality inhaler technique instruction to patients. Encouraging a patient to bring their own devices to clinic is a useful alternative.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 62
NON-PHARMACOLOGICAL TREATMENT OF STABLE COPD
Non-pharmacological treatment is complementary to pharmacological treatment and should form part of the comprehensive management of COPD.
After receiving a diagnosis of COPD a patient should be given further information about the condition. Physicians should emphasize the importance of a smoke free environment, empower adherence to prescribed medication, ensure proper inhaler technique, promote physical activity, prescribe vaccinations, and refer patients to pulmonary rehabilitation.
Algorithms for the initiation and follow-up of non- pharmacological treatment E
Some relevant non-pharmacological measures based on the patient’s GOLD A, BR, E group AT DIAGNOSIS are
T summarized in Figure 3.12. S
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 63
Recommendations for FOLLOW UP non-pharmacological treatments are based on a patient’s treatable traits e.g., symptoms and exacerbations (Figure 3.13).
Rehabilitation, Education & Self-Management
Pulmonary rehabilitation
Pulmonary rehabilitation is defined as “a comprehensive intervention based on thorough patient assessment followed by patient-tailored therapies that include, but are not limited to, exercise training, education, self-management intervention aiming at behavior change, designed to improve the physical and psychological condition of people with chronic respiratory disease and to promote the long-term adherence to health-enhancing behaviors.”(750,751)
Patients with high symptom burden and risk of exacerbations (Groups B and E), should be encouraged to take part in a formal rehabilitation program that includes setting patient goals and is designed and delivered in a structured manner, taking into account the individual’s COPD characteristics and comorbidities.(750,752,753) This includes patients who are older, female, more deprived, or have a comorbidity of diabetes, asthma, or painful condition and currently appear less likely to be referred for pulmonary rehabilitation.(754)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 64
Pulmonary rehabilitation should be considered as part of integrated patient management, and usually includes a range of healthcare professionals to ensure optimum coverage of the many aspects involved.(752) Optimum benefits are achieved from programs lasting 6 to 8 weeks. Available evidence indicates that there are no additional benefits from extending pulmonary rehabilitation to 12 weeks.(755) Supervised exercise training at least twice weekly is recommended, and this can include any regimen from endurance training, interval training, resistance/strength training; upper and lower limbs ideally should be included as well as walking exercise; flexibility, inspiratory muscle training and neuromuscular electrical stimulation can also be incorporated. In all cases the rehabilitation intervention
(content, scope, frequency, and intensity) should be individualized to maximize personal functional gains.(755) If a center based pulmonary rehabilitation program is complimented with a physical activity promotion program, clinically significant improvements in physical activity are observed.(756,757) The importance of long-term behavior change to improve physical functionality, and reduce the psychological impact of COPD, should be emphasized to the patient.
Assessment and follow-up of pulmonary rehabilitation
Baseline and outcome assessments of each participant in a pulmonary rehabilitation prograEm should be made to
T specify individual maladaptive behaviors (including motivation), physical and mental healtUh impediments to training,
B goals, barriers and capabilities and to quantify gains and to target areas for improvement.
Assessments should include: I
► Detailed history and physical examination O
► Measurement of post-bronchodilator spirometry
► Assessment of exercise capacity O
► Measurement of health status and impact of breathl essness
► Assessment of inspiratory and expiratory muscle strength and lower limb strength in patients who suffer
N from muscle wasting O
► Discussion about individual patient goals aDnd expectations
The first two assessments are important for Iestablishing entry suitability and baseline status but are not used in
R outcome assessment. E
Exercise tolerance can be assessed by cycle ergometry or treadmill exercise with the measurement of a number of
T physiological variables, includiHng maximum oxygen consumption, maximum heart rate, and maximum work
G performed. Standardized self-paced, timed walking tests (e.g., 6-minute walking distance) are useful in clinical practice
R as they require minimal faYcilities and are relevant to routine functioning. Shuttle walking tests provide more complete
P information than an Oentirely self-paced test, and are simpler to perform than a treadmill test.(758) Walking tests do
C require at least one practice session before data can be interpreted.
It is important not to limit assessment only to these outcome measures but gather information on each patient’s ultimate goal (relevant or valued outcomes), such as their desired achievements in work, home and leisure by the end of the program.
Several detailed questionnaires for assessing health status are available, including some specifically designed for patients with respiratory disease. Health status can also be assessed by generic instruments, although these are less sensitive to change than the disease specific questionnaires such as the CAT™, CRQ or SGRQ. The Hospital Anxiety and
Depression Scale (HADS)(759) and the Primary Care Evaluation of Mental Disorders (PRIME-MD) Patient
Questionnaire(760) have been used to improve identification and treatment of anxious and depressed patients.

Education, self-management and integrative care
Patient “education” often takes the form of providers giving information and advice, and assumes that knowledge will lead to behavior change. Although enhancing patient knowledge is an important step towards behavior change, didactic group sessions are insufficient for promoting self-management skills. Topics such as smoking cessation, correct use of inhaler devices, early recognition of exacerbation, decision-making and taking action, and when to seek help, surgical interventions, considering advance directives, and others will be better dealt with using self- management interventions. Personalized education and training that considers specific issues relating to the individual patients, and that aims to enhance long-term functionality and appropriate health behaviors, is likely to benefit patients more. These are addressed under self-management.
Education
Patients may have individual and/or group education sessions. During group sessions, patients engage in active, participatory-based learning of program content. During one-on-one interactions, a motivational communication style
E should be used, as this approach empowers patients to take greater responsibility for theTir health and wellbeing,
U where physicians and other healthcare professionals only serve as guides in the behaviorB change process.
Topics considered appropriate for an education program include: smoking cessatiSon; basic information about COPD;
D general approach to therapy and specific aspects of medical treatment (respiratory medications and inhalation
R devices); strategies to help minimize dyspnea; advice about when to seek hOelp; decision-making during exacerbations; and advance directives and end-of-life issues. The intensity and contYent of these educational messages will vary
P depending on the severity of the patient’s disease, although Othe specific contributions of education to the
C improvements seen after pulmonary rehabilitation remain unclear.(761) Implicit in this description is the provision of
“self-management support/coaching”, which refers to theO strategies, techniques and skills used by healthcare
N providers to arm patients with the knowledge, confidence and skills required to self-manage their disease effectively.
However, the individual patient’s evaluation and risDk assessment with respect to exacerbations, patient’s needs, preferences, and personal goals should inform the p-ersonalized design of the self-management education plan.
Self-management
Self-management education and coachAing by healthcare professionals should be a major component of the “Chronic
Care Model” within the context of the healthcare delivery system.
The aim of self-management interventions is to motivate, engage and coach patients to positively adapt their health
R behavior(s) and develop Yskills to better manage their COPD on a day-to-day basis.(762) Physicians and healthcare
P providers need to go beyond pure education/advice-giving (didactic) approaches to help patients learn and adopt
O sustainable self-maCnagement skills. The basis of enabling patients to become active partners in their ongoing care is to build knowledge and skills. It is important to recognize that patient education alone does not itself change behavior or even motivate patients, and it has had no impact on improving exercise performance or lung function,(763,764) but it can play a role in improving skills, ability to cope with illness, and health status.(750)
Integrative care
COPD is a complex disease that requires the input of multiple care providers who need to work together closely. In principle, use of a formal structured program that determines how each component is delivered should make care more efficient and effective, but the evidence for this is divided.

Delivery of pulmonary rehabilitation, education & self-management: in-person versus virtual
The COVID-19 pandemic and resulting need for social distancing forced the healthcare system to implement remote solutions and telehealth to ensure delivery of appropriate care. However, the use and acceptance of telehealth solutions in the post-pandemic context is still being elucidated. To date the most studied telehealth modalities in COPD are pulmonary rehabilitation and self-management interventions.
Pulmonary rehabilitation has the highest quality studies in the COPD-telehealth field with well-defined and reported exercise training programs, and comparing telerehabilitation with usual care and/or center-based rehabilitation, which has been established as the gold-standard in a 2021 Cochrane review.(765) A clinical practice guideline reported that telerehabilitation can achieve similar clinical outcomes as face-to-face, center-based pulmonary rehabilitation, and recommended that adults with stable chronic respiratory disease, particularly COPD, should be offered the choice of a center-based pulmonary rehabilitation or telerehabilitation (strong recommendation, moderate quality
E evidence).(751) T
Key to the delivery of an effective pulmonary rehabilitation program, with demonstrated proven benefits for COPD, is
T the inclusion of lower extremity endurance exercise training. For most people telSerehabilitation still involves a face I
D to-face, center-based assessment to complete a comprehensive pre-rehabilitation evaluation. Telerehabilitation does
R create a means to provide pulmonary rehabilitation services and, likOe center-based rehabilitation, it requires appropriately trained staff and adequate infrastructure. However, consYidering issues such as lack of trained medical
P personnel, and the appeal of technology, there is a need to ensurOe that telehealth pulmonary rehabilitation is not
C misused.
A 2022 Cochrane review reported that a self-management intervention both improved quality of life and reduced
O hospital admissions.(766) Tele-education and self-manDagement using information communication technology could have the potential to ease the working life of health - practitioners and transform the way patients are monitored, and
L healthcare is delivered. A
Despite the many advances in self-management, delivering self-management at distance using digital technology still
A raises many unanswered questions aMnd important limitations. No studies to date have been designed to demonstrate
T whether there is any additional benefit of telehealth-supported self-management compared to self-management
H interventions not using informGation communication technologies.(767)
Oxygen therapy and ventilatory support
Oxygen therapy
The long-term administration of oxygen (> 15 hours per day) to patients with chronic respiratory failure has been shown to increase survival in patients with severe resting hypoxemia.(768)
Ekström and colleagues(769) reported that among patients with severe hypoxemia agreeing to participate in a study, long-term oxygen therapy used for 24 hours per day did not result in a lower risk of hospitalization or death within one year compared to therapy for 15 hours per day, but the generalizability of this finding is unclear.
Long-term oxygen therapy does not lengthen time to death or first hospitalization or provide sustained benefit for any of the measured outcomes in patients with stable COPD and resting or exercise-induced moderate arterial oxygen
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 67 desaturation.(770) Breathlessness may be relieved in COPD patients who are either mildly hypoxemic, or non-hypoxemic but do not otherwise qualify for home oxygen therapy, when oxygen is given during exercise training; however, studies have shown no improvement of breathlessness in daily life and no benefit on health-related quality of life (Figure
3.14).(770-772) There are contradictory studies although the majority do not demonstrate changes.(773)
Although air travel is safe for most paMtients with chronic respiratory failure who are on long-term oxygen therapy,(774) patients should ideally maintain aTn in-flight PaO of at least 6.7 kPa (50 mmHg). Studies indicate that this can be
H achieved in those with moderGate to severe hypoxemia at sea level by supplementary oxygen at 3 liters/min by nasal cannula or 31% by VenturiR fa I cemask.(775) Those with a resting oxygen saturation > 95% and 6-minute walk oxygen
Y saturation > 84% may Ptravel without further assessment,(776) although it is important to emphasize that resting
O oxygenation at sea level does not exclude the development of severe hypoxemia when travelling by air.(774) Careful
C consideration should be given to any comorbidity that may impair oxygen delivery to tissues (e.g., cardiac impairment, anemia). Also, walking along the aisle may profoundly aggravate hypoxemia.(777)
Long-term oxygen therapy (LTOT) is indicated for stable patients who have:
► PaO at or below 55 mmHg (7.3 kPa) or SaO at or below 88%, with or without hypercapnia confirmed
2 2 twice over a three-week period; or
► PaO between 55 mmHg (7.3 kPa) and 60 mmHg (8.0 kPa), or SaO of 88%, if there is evidence of pulmonary
2 2 hypertension, peripheral edema suggesting congestive cardiac failure, or polycythemia (hematocrit >
Once placed on LTOT the patient should be re-evaluated after 60 to 90 days with repeat arterial blood gas (ABG) or oxygen saturation measurements while inspiring room air and the level of oxygen flow that had been prescribed to determine if oxygen is still indicated and if so, therapeutic. Ventilatory support
Noninvasive ventilation (NYIV) is occasionally used in patients with stable very severe COPD.(778) NIV may be considered
P of some use in a seleOcted group of patients, particularly in those with pronounced daytime hypercapnia and recent hospitalization, althCough a systematic review was unable to support or refute this.(779) In contrast, in patients with both
COPD and obstructive sleep apnea there are clear indications for continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP).(780,781)
During exacerbations of COPD
NIV in the form of noninvasive positive pressure ventilation (NPPV) is the standard of care for decreasing morbidity and mortality in patients hospitalized with an exacerbation of COPD and acute respiratory failure(782-785) (see also
Chapter 4).
Stable patient
In patients with both COPD and obstructive sleep apnea there are clear benefits associated with the use of continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) to improve both survival and the risk of hospital admissions.(780,781)
Whether to use NPPV chronically at home to treat patients with acute on chronic respiratory failure following
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 69 hospitalization remains undetermined and outcome may be affected by persistent hypercapnia.(786) A multicenter prospective RCT of COPD patients with persistent hypercapnia (PaCO > 53 mmHg) after 2-4 weeks of hospital discharge because an acute episode of exacerbation, compared the effects of home NIV plus oxygen compared to home oxygen alone on time to readmission or death.(786) Results showed that adding home NIV to oxygen therapy significantly prolonged the time to readmission or death within 12 months.(786) A systematic review and meta-analysis of these studies confirms that NIV decreases mortality and risk of hospitalization. The best candidate subgroups (by recent hospitalization history or PaCO ) remain unclear.(785)
Two previous retrospective studies(787,788) and two of three RCTs(786,789-792) reported reductions in re-hospitalization and improved survival with using NPPV post-hospitalization. Two studies reported decreases in mortality and hospitalization rates while another showed no benefit of NPPV for survival.(790) Several factors may account for discrepancies: differences in patient selection, underpowered studies, NPPV settings incapable of achieving adequate ventilation, and poor adherence with NPPV therapy.(793) NPPV when indicated should be instituted and monitored under the direction of personnel familiar with the process and the devices utilized.(794,795)
A meta-analysis suggested that in patients with chronic hypercapnic respiratory failure lonBg-term treatment with HFNC
R may reduce acute exacerbations and improve SGRQ scores compared to standard care, but its effect on mortality and
T hospitalizations is uncertain.(796,797) S
MONITORING AND FOLLOW-UP Y
Routine follow-up of COPD patients is essential. Lung function m ay worsen over time, even with the best available
O care. Symptoms, exacerbations and objective measures of airflow obstruction should be monitored to determine
N when to modify management and to identify any complica tions and/or comorbidities that may develop.
 Symptoms L
At each visit, information on symptoms sincIe the last visit should be collected, including cough and sputum,
R breathlessness, fatigue, activity limitation,E and sleep disturbances, including insomnia. Questionnaires such as the
COPD Assessment Test (CAT™)(476) can bAe used; trends and changes are more valuable than single measurements.
Exacerbations
The frequency, severity, tyRpeI and likely causes of all exacerbations(798) should be monitored. Sputum volume and
Y presence or absence of sputum purulence should be noted. Specific inquiry into response to previous treatment,
O unscheduled visits to providers, telephone calls for assistance, and use of urgent or emergency care facilities is
C important. Hospitalizations should be documented, including the facility, duration of stay, and any use of critical care or mechanical ventilatory support.
Adherence and appropriate use of prescribed treatments
This is a key action in the chronic management of COPD patients that should be mandatory in each clinical visit. The following aspects need careful and personalized attention:
► Dosages of prescribed medications
► Adherence to the regimen
► Inhaler technique
► Effectiveness of the current regime
► Side effects
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 70
Treatment modifications should be recommended (Figure 3.7).
Smoking status
At each visit, the current smoking status and smoke exposure should be determined followed by appropriate action.
Measurements
Decline in FEV1 can be tracked by spirometry performed at regular intervals (e.g., yearly) to identify patients who are declining quickly, although other lung function parameters reflecting hyperinflation and gas transfer may also be informative.
A timed walking test (6-minute walking distance or shuttle-walking test) provides additional information regarding prognosis.(799,800) Measurement of oxygenation at rest in an arterial blood gas sample may help identify patients who will benefit from supplemental oxygen to improve both symptoms and survival in those with severe resting hypoxemia.
Imaging U
If there is a clear worsening of symptoms, imaging may be indicated. When exacerbatioRnsI are repeatedly characterized
T by purulent sputum, patients should be investigated for bronchiectasis.
Comorbidities R
Symptoms that may indicate the development or worsening of a comorYbi d condition such as lung cancer, obstructive
P sleep apnea, congestive heart failure, ischemic heart disease, osteoporosis or depression/anxiety etc. should be
O recorded. If present, an appropriate diagnostic work-up should foCllow (see also Chapter 5).
Telehealth, remote monitoring and follow-upN
The COVID-19 pandemic has dramatically changed howD outpatient care is delivered in health care practices. Telehealth
- may offer a bridge to care, and now offers a chanLce to consider virtual and hybrid virtual/in-person care models, with
A a goal of improved healthcare access, outcomIes, and affordability.(801) However, incorporation of virtual care into
R ambulatory care should be based on evidenEce.
From a recent Cochrane review(802) on telehealth for remote monitoring and consultations for patients with COPD,
T different models have been revieHwed based on RCTs:
► Remote monYitoring (linked to a healthcare professional) plus usual care versus usual care alone (as
P reportedO by trialists).
► Remote consultation (e.g., real-time contact with a health professional) plus usual care versus usual care alone (e.g., face-to- face visit for a check-up in a health service with a health professional, or as reported by trialists).
► Remote monitoring or remote consultation versus usual care (e.g., where tele healthcare has replaced an element of usual face-to-face care).
For most of the studies included in the review (24 RCTs) remote monitoring interventions required participants to transfer measurements using a remote device for subsequent health professional review (asynchronous) as opposed to only 5 RCTs that transferred data and allowed review by health professionals in real time (synchronous).(802) The results of this systematic review demonstrate the paucity of evidence of superiority of these models compared to usual care, i.e., exacerbations, hospitalization, health status and mortality. While there was no evidence of harm, it is still unclear which COPD severity subgroups would benefit, or possibly be harmed, by telehealth interventions.
Telehealth interventions may be beneficial as an additional health resource based on professional assessment, and
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 71 depending on individual needs, although the long-term effects remain unknown.
Information is provided below on how i) to prepare for the remote visit; ii) to set up the visit agenda with the patient; and a standardized checklist is provided for follow-up of COPD patients, whether in-person, by phone or in a virtual/online setting, in Appendix 1 at the end of the GOLD report.
The principles of good record keeping and clinical practice should always apply: i) treat patients with dignity; ii) respect people’s right to privacy and confidentiality; iii) listen to the patient’s needs and act in their best interest; and iv) base your recommendations on the best available evidence.
Triage and prioritizing process
The process of triage should help decide: i) whether to offer an in-person as opposed to a remote (telephone or virtual/online) consultation, and ii) who to prioritize.
Remote follow-up could be considered in the following situations: U
• Patient or caregiver can understand the process and provide information clearlyI;
• Regular COPD follow-up or patient followed for a known condition; T
• Medical records and laboratory test results are accessible to the healthDcaIre professionals;
• Prescription and access to medication is possible and follow-up Rto the prescription can be arranged if
O necessary.
In-person follow-up should be prioritized in these situations: C
• Patient and caregiver have difficulty providing informat T ion;
• Patient needs immediate attention due to the presNence of severe medical symptoms;
• Changes in patient’s symptoms require a differential diagnosis work-up with the need for a physical exam
D and/or laboratory testing;  L
• Patient treatment can only be given in pAerson and cannot be given at home.
Prioritization of in-person visits should taTke into consideration the COPD patient disease severity (symptom burden
A and risk of exacerbations), recent eMmergency department visit and/or hospital admission, associated significant comorbidities, age, and/or living aTlone at home.
