 Management of mucus hypersecretionL
Treatment goals for patient with chronic bronchitis (CB) include: 1) reducing the overproduction of mucus; 2)
E decreasing mucus hypersecretion by redTucing inflammation; 3) facilitating elimination of mucus by increasing ciliary
A transport; 4) decreasing mucus viscosMity; and 5) facilitating cough mechanisms. Smoking cessation can improve cough by improving mucociliary function Tand decreasing goblet cell hyperplasia.(1091) Smoking cessation may decrease airway
H injury by limiting immune mGechanisms that cause persistent inflammation and abnormal epithelial cell gene
I expression.(1092) R
Mucus clearance tCreatments that promote mechanical movement through the airway such as oscillating positive expiratory pressure (OPEP) therapy may improve mucus mobilization, symptoms and quality of life in people with
COPD who produce sputum daily or most days.(1093,1094) The use of nebulized hypertonic saline for copious mucus has been used in obstructive lung disease and cystic fibrosis with beneficial effects. However, in patients with COPD, current studies are limited, and results are inconsistent (1095-1099)
Long-acting muscarinic antagonists, predominantly tiotropium and aclidinium, can improve sputum production and decrease cough in patients with moderate to severe COPD.(1100-1103) Triple therapy with dual long-acting bronchodilators combined with inhaled steroids may be effective in reducing exacerbations and improving lung function and quality of life regardless of the presence of mucus hypersecretion.
Use of mucolytics was associated with a reduction of 0.03 exacerbations per participant per month compared with placebo, that is, about 0.36 per year, or one exacerbation every three years. Very high heterogeneity was noted for this outcome, so results need to be interpreted with caution.(1032) Nevertheless, in participants with chronic bronchitis
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 94 or COPD, we are moderately confident that treatment with mucolytics may produce a small reduction in acute exacerbations and a small effect on overall quality of life.(1032) Recombinant human DNase has similarly shown lack of benefit in mucopurulent patients with COPD.(1104,1105) New classes of mucolytics agents are being developed.(1106) In a small double-blind placebo-controlled study, patients randomized to receive a CFTR potentiator icenticaftor had improvements in FEV1 and sputum bacterial colonization compared to placebo.(1107) New bronchoscopic interventions have been proposed to reduce mucus hypersecretion by eliminating airway goblet cell hyperplasia and submucosal glands. Liquid nitrogen metered cryospray, rheoplasty, and targeted lung denervation are currently under evaluation.(1108-1111)
Overview of the evidence: Non-pharmacological therapy
Self-management
A Delphi process has resulted in a conceptual definition for COPD self-management intUerventions: “A COPD self B management intervention is structured but personalized and often multi-component, with goals of motivating,
R engaging and supporting the patients to positively adapt their health behavior(s) anTd develop skills to better manage
S their disease.”(762) The process requires iterative interactions between patients anId healthcare professionals who are
D competent in delivering self-management interventions. Behavior change techniques are used to elicit patient
R motivation, confidence and competence. Literacy sensitive approaches areO used to enhance comprehensibility.(762)
Systematic reviews have provided evidence that self-management interventions improve outcomes in COPD. A 2022
Cochrane review reported that interventions for people with CO PD are associated with improvements in HRQoL, a
O lower probability of respiratory-related hospital admissions, and no excess respiratory-related and all-cause mortality
N risks.(766) This strengthens the view that self-manageOme nt interventions are unlikely to cause harm. There had previously been concerns that health benefits from seDlf-management programs in COPD could be counterbalanced by
- increased mortality.(1112,1113) However, a previousL C ochrane review and another meta-analysis reported no impact of
A self-management interventions on overall morItality, and while the Cochrane review did find a small, but statistically
R significant, higher respiratory-related morEtality rate in the self-management invention group as compared to usual
T care, the authors of the review stated Athe results should be interpreted with caution as misclassification in cause of
M death is common, the overall effect was dominated by two studies, and no effect on all-cause mortality was seen in
T the overall analysis. FurthermorHe, two independent, well-designed studies, the COMET(1114) and the PIC-COPD,(1115)
G have shown the potential for reduction in mortality from integrated case management with self-management
R interventions. The prograYm in these two studies may have promoted earlier appropriate treatment for exacerbations,
P which could have preOvented some fatal complications. These data, in conjunction with the most recently published
Cochrane review, once again strengthens the view that self-management interventions are unlikely to cause harm.(766)
An RCT has shown that implementation of a comprehensive 3-month program to improve long-term self-management of patients recently discharged from hospital with COPD exacerbation resulted in nearly two-fold higher rates of COPD- related hospitalizations and emergency visits over 6 months. These data suggest that self-management strategies in recently hospitalized patients may lead to increased health care service utilization compared with usual care.(1116)
There remain problems with heterogeneity among interventions, consistency of their application, specifics of the intervention, patient populations, follow-up times and outcome measures that make generalization difficult in real life. It is also challenging to formulate clear recommendations regarding the most effective form and content of a self- management intervention in COPD given the range of heterogeneity across studies, and lack of precise definitions of self-management components (e.g., skills taught) and fidelity measures. The recent conceptual definition should help redress these deficiencies. For example, in the definition it is mentioned that: “The process requires iterative
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 95 interactions between patients and healthcare professionals who are competent in delivering self-management interventions.” Having proper health coaching is important to improve self-management abilities. In people with
COPD admitted for an exacerbation, a study has reported the positive effect of health coaching, commencing at the time of hospital discharge, on reducing risk of re-hospitalization and emergency department visits.(1117) Furthermore, this randomized study indicated that health coaching delivered by a respiratory therapist or nurse may improve self- management abilities as demonstrated by meaningful improvements in Chronic Respiratory Disease Questionnaire mastery scores.(1118)
Integrated care programs
COPD is a complex disease that requires the input of multiple care providers who need to work together closely. In principle, use of a formal structured program that determines how each component is delivered should make care more efficient and effective, but the evidence for this is divided. Currently available evidence is conflicting regarding the benefits of integrated care/disease management programs in terms of quality of life, exercise capacity, and use of healthcare resources.(1119-1121) Besides, delivering integrated interventions by telemedicine didE not show a significant
T effect.(1122,1123) The pragmatic conclusion is that well organized care is important, but therUe may be no advantage in
B structuring it tightly into a formalized program. Furthermore, integrated care needs to Ibe individualized to the stage
R of the person’s illness and health literacy. T
Physical activity R
Pulmonary rehabilitation, including community and home-based, is aYn approach with clear evidence of benefits.
However, the challenge is promoting physical activity and maintaiOning it. There is evidence that physical activity is
C decreased in COPD patients.(1124) This leads to a downward spiral of inactivity which predisposes patients to reduced
T quality of life, increased rates of hospitalization and mortalityO.(1125-1127) As such, there has been tremendous interest in
N implementing behavior-targeted interventions with the aim of improving physical activity(1128) and these should be
O encouraged.(1125) Technology-based interventions havDe the potential to provide convenient and accessible means to enhance exercise self-efficacy, and to educate a-nd motivate people in their efforts to make healthy lifestyle
L changes.(1129) The use of an internet-mediatedA intervention may benefit people with COPD with low baseline self I
R efficacy to increase physical activity.(1130) However, most published studies to date provide little guidance, being
E inconsistent in the techniques, and lackinTg the necessary details (e.g., type, quantity, timing and method of delivery;
A tools used; quality-assurance methodMs) to replicate the study or adapt the interventions for clinical care. One RCT that
T evaluated the long-term effectiveness of a community-based physical activity coaching intervention in people with
COPD exacerbation history shGowed no benefits in acute care use or survival.(1131) Another pedometer-based physical
R activity interventional study (pedometer alone or pedometer plus a website with feedback) showed an association
Y between the interventPion and reduced risk for acute exacerbations over 12-15 months of follow-up.(1132) Non O pharmacological inCterventions such as pursed lip breathing and diaphragmatic breathing have also been shown to improve pulmonary function and increased exercise capacity in patients with COPD.(1133)
Exercise training
A meta-analysis of RCTs found that exercise training alone, or with the addition of activity counseling, significantly improved physical activity levels in COPD patients.(1134) A combination of constant load or interval training with strength training provides better outcomes than either method alone.(1135)
Where possible, endurance exercise training to 60-80% of the symptom-limited maximum work or heart rate is preferred,(1136) or to a Borg-rated dyspnea or fatigue score of 4 to 6 (moderate to severe).(1137) Endurance training can be accomplished through either continuous or interval exercise programs. The latter involves the patient doing the same total work but divided into briefer periods of high-intensity exercise, a useful strategy when performance is limited by other comorbidities.(1138,1139)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 96
In some cultures, other alternatives such as Tai Chi practice, emphasizing the use of ‘mind’ or concentration for control of breathing and circular body movement, has been shown to improve exercise capacity in comparison to usual care in COPD patients.(1140) However, from this meta-analysis, the effects of Tai Chi in reducing dyspnea level and improving quality of life remain inconclusive. Future studies addressing these topics and the most beneficial protocols for Tai Chi practice are warranted.
Exercise training can be enhanced by optimizing bronchodilators,(923) since both LAMA and LABA have shown reduced resting and dynamic hyperinflation. These changes contribute to better training effects.(306,1141) Adding strength training to aerobic training is effective in improving strength, but does not improve health status or exercise tolerance.(1142) Upper extremities exercise training improves arm strength and endurance, and results in improved functional capacity for upper extremity activities.(1143) Exercise capacity may also be improved by whole-body vibration training.(1144)
Inspiratory muscle training increases strength of inspiratory muscles,(1145) but this not consistently translate to better
E performance, reduced dyspnea or improved health related quality of life when added to a coTmprehensive pulmonary
U rehabilitation program.(1146-1148) B
Pulmonary rehabilitation S
The benefits to COPD patients from pulmonary rehabilitation are consideraRble (Figure 3.24), and rehabilitation has
O been shown to be the most effective therapeutic strategy to improve sho rtness of breath, health status and exercise
Y tolerance.(1149) Pulmonary rehabilitation is appropriate for most peoPple with COPD; improved functional exercise
O capacity and health related quality of life have been demonstratCed across all grades of COPD severity, although the evidence is especially strong in patients with moderate to seTvere disease. Even patients with chronic hypercapnic
O failure show benefit.(1150,1151) N
Exercise-induced oxygen desaturation can be seen in a significant minority of COPD patients and has been associated
- with impaired quality of life, exacerbation risk, aLnd mortality.(1152) A large RCT did not suggest clinical improvement
A with long term oxygen therapy for patientRs Iwithout resting hypoxemia but exertional desaturation.(1153) During
E pulmonary rehabilitation it is common practice to supplement oxygen during exercise training with the aim of
A facilitating higher exercise intensity. There was little support for oxygen supplementation during exercise training for
M individuals with COPD from a 2007 s ystematic review,(1154) but most evidence was limited by low study quality. A large
RCT,(773) with blinding of participants, trainers and assessors, demonstrated that COPD patients training with either
G supplemental oxygen or meRdIical air had significantly improved exercise capacity and health-related quality of life; no
Y greater benefit with oxygen was observed. The incidence and severity of adverse events were similar in both groups.
In patients with severOe COPD on long-term oxygen therapy (LTOT) in whom exercise training is done with oxygenation
C systems, there has been increased interest in using an alternative tool, namely nasally administered mixtures of humidified air-oxygen blends at flow rates of 20-60 L/min (HFNT). HFNT may reduce respiratory muscle load and respiratory rate, while increasing expiratory time.(1155) In an RCT, the delivery of HFNT during training sessions, as compared with usual oxygen, was not associated with a greater improvement in endurance time, the primary outcome, or in health status.(1156) However, a greater improvement in 6-minute walking distance (6MWD) test was observed with HFNT. A similar small trial suggested an improved walking distance.(1157) The proportion of patients reaching the minimal clinically important difference (MCID) in endurance time and 6MWD was also significantly higher with HFNT. Finally, there was no significant difference between the two therapies in patients’ satisfaction. Further studies are needed to evaluate the efficacy of this treatment.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 97
There are data from large RCTs regaMrding the effectiveness of pulmonary rehabilitation after hospitalization for an acute exacerbation of COPD. A sysTtematic review that included 13 RCTs reported reduced mortality, and number of
H readmissions among patients Gwho had pulmonary rehabilitation initiated during hospitalization or within 4 weeks of
I discharge.(848) Long-term efRfects on mortality were not statistically significant, but improvements in health-related
Y quality of life and exerPcise capacity appeared to be maintained for at least 12 months. These results have been
O corroborated by reaCl world evidence, from a large population-based cohort of more than 190,000 patients hospitalized for COPD in the US, in whom initiation of pulmonary rehabilitation within 90 days of discharge, while rare, was significantly associated with lower risk of mortality(851) and fewer rehospitalizations at one year.(1158) One study has reported that initiating pulmonary rehabilitation before the patient’s discharge may compromise survival through unknown mechanisms.(1159) Pulmonary rehabilitation ranks as one of the most cost-effective treatment strategies.(752)
There are many challenges with pulmonary rehabilitation. Referral of patients who might benefit, uptake and completion of pulmonary rehabilitation is frequently limited, partly through provider ignorance as well as patients’ lack of awareness of availability or benefits. The recommended length of pulmonary rehabilitation (minimum of 6 weeks) could also be a limitation in many countries due to funding constraints of insurance companies and/or national health funds. Virtual reality pulmonary rehabilitation could be an alternative combined or not with traditional exercise training; this may be of particular interest in countries where the length of pulmonary rehabilitation programs is limited to less than 4 weeks.(1160) Another challenge is encouraging sustained long-term physical activity. Although the approach may need to be personalized, behavioral lifestyle physical activity intervention has shown promising results
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 98 i.e., the potential to decrease sedentarity and increase physical activity in patients with moderate to severe COPD.(1161)
A major barrier to full participation is access, which is particularly limited by geography, culture, finances, transport and other logistics.(750,800,807,1162)
Pulmonary rehabilitation can be conducted at a range of sites.(750) Community-based and home-based programs have been shown to be as effective as hospital-based programs in randomized controlled trials,(1163,1164) as long as the frequency and intensity are equivalent.(1165) In countries where there is economic limitation or those with challenges because patients live in rural or remote regions, home-based programs that deliver exercise training using a stationary bicycle(1163) or a walking program(1164) could be considered as alternative to traditional hospital rehabilitation training programs. There is also evidence that standardized home-based pulmonary rehabilitation programs improve dyspnea in COPD patients.(1166) However, in real life, traditional pulmonary rehabilitation with supervision remains the standard of care and first-line option, with home-based exercise likely to be a less effective alternative for people with COPD who are unable to attend pulmonary rehabilitation.(1167) Another challenge is that the benefits of rehabilitation tend to wane over time. There is insufficient evidence, with conflicting research findings in the 11 available RCTs, to
E recommend continuation of lower intensity or lower frequency exercise programs with the aiTm of maintaining benefit
U long-term. However, if such programs are available, they should target health behavBior taking into account the
R patient’s own preferences, needs and personal goals.(755,1168) Pulmonary rehabilitation may help reduce anxiety and
T depression symptoms.(1169) S
Tele-rehabilitation O
In- or out-patient pulmonary rehabilitation (PR) in COPD is effePctive in improving several clinically relevant
O outcomes.(1149,1170) There is clear evidence that core componentCs of PR including exercise training combined with disease-specific education and self-management interventions(T750,1149) can benefit almost every COPD patient.(1171-1173)
However, there are many challenges encountered in thOe delivery of PR, which include systemic barriers integral to
D some health care systems leading to a scarcity of in-p erson PR programs and facilities. In many regions, the programs
- that do exist tend to be located in urban areas. HLence attending PR is challenging for many COPD patients. Even for
A those patients residing in urban areas, availaRbiIlity of frequent transportation that is required for out-patient PR may
E still be a challenge.
Tele-rehabilitation has been propos ed as an alternative to the traditional approaches. This has become even more
H relevant in the COVID-19 pandemic era where in-person PR has not been feasible, and models of delivery had to be
G adapted. However, it is impRoIrtant to distinguish between evidence-based tele-rehabilitation models and pandemic Y adapted models. Most of the available evidence regarding tele-rehabilitation has been analyzed in a recent Cochrane
P review.(765) O
Across multiple trials performed in groups and individuals with a large variety of tele-rehabilitation delivery platforms
(videoconferencing, telephone only, website with telephone support, mobile application with feedback, centralized
“hub” for people to come together), the reported results suggest that tele-rehabilitation is safe and has similar benefits to those of center-based PR across a range of outcomes. The evidence-based models from the Cochrane review were published before the COVID-19 pandemic, and have all included an in-person exercise test at the center prior to commencement, for the purposes of assessing the full extent of desaturation during exercise training(1174) and accurately prescribing exercise capacity.(1175)
Promoting physical activity is also central to the recommendations that should be given to every COPD patient regardless of disease severity. Promoting physical activity using smartphone apps is gaining popularity although at present data on effectiveness are lacking.(1176)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 99
In the field of tele-rehabilitation, the evidence base is still evolving and best practices are not yet established at this time due to a lack of: i) standardization of delivery platform, e.g., no one single best mode of tele-rehabilitation delivery; ii) tests performed remotely allowing for accurate exercise prescription; iii) information on suitable variations in components and timing of interventions (e.g., no data are available regarding post-exacerbation rehabilitation); and iv) evidence about duration of benefit (beyond immediate post PR). Furthermore, it is unclear what types of patients were recruited to these studies or their level of familiarity with the technology used. In order to ensure that PR is accessible to all, we must understand the barriers that might be unique to tele-rehabilitation.
Nutritional support
In people with COPD, weight loss and malnutrition develop as disease severity progresses and indicates a poor prognosis. Malnutrition in COPD is associated with impaired lung function, increased hospitalizations, poor exercise tolerance, worsened quality of life and increased mortality.(360,1177-1181) Malnutrition has been reported in 30-60% of patients hospitalized with COPD;(1182) up to 50% of people with COPD weigh less than 90% of ideal body weight.(1183)
Weight loss occurs when energy expenditure exceeds energy supply; in people with COPD decEreases in appetite and
T oral intake often coincide with elevated systemic levels of pro-inflammatory cytokines andU the appetite suppressant
B hormone, leptin.(1184,1185) The severity of airflow obstruction correlates with the presenIce of malnutrition(1186) since
R ventilator inefficiency increases daily energy requirements.(1187) The imbalance of deTcreased oral intake and increased
S energy expenditure can lead to a negative nitrogen balance and decreases in skDeIletal muscle mass and function.(1188 1190) R
Nutritional repletion in people with COPD should be coupled with optPimization of lung function, regular exercise, and
O improvement of tissue oxygenation. Dietary advice and oral supplementation have been reported to improve body
C weight, quality of life, respiratory muscle strength and 6-minuTte walk distance.(826,1182) However, nutritional support
O has not been consistently shown to improve lung function.(826,1191-1193) Multimodality treatment that incorporates
N rehabilitation with nutritional support and protein supOpl ementation may improve fat free mass, BMI and exercise
D performance.(1194) Among malnourished, hospitalized people with COPD, a protein enriched supplementation
- decreased mortality and improved handgrip strenLg th, body weight and nutritional biomarkers 90 days post hospital
A discharge.(1195) I

INTERVENTIONAL & SURGICAL THERAPIES FOR COPD
COPD is associated with airway and lung parenchyma structural changes that provide potential targets for interventional and surgical treatments to alleviate dyspnea, reduce cough and mucus production, and improve quality of life (Figure 3.25).
Lung structural related thYerapies for COPD include airway and emphysematous predominant treatments. Phenotyping
P patients with clinical,O physiological, and imaging tests is critical to select appropriate candidates and in assessing the
C benefits, timing, and type of intervention to be performed. Multidisciplinary collaboration of pulmonology, thoracic surgery and imaging disciplines are necessary to ensure quality outcomes.
Airway predominant treatments are currently the subject of Phase III clinical trials; emphysematous based treatments include bullectomy, lung volume reduction surgery, bronchoscopic lung reduction and in select cases, lung transplantation. Each of these therapies are reviewed below.
Surgical and interventional treatments for patients with emphysema depends upon the severity of patient symptoms despite optimized medical treatment, the specific structural abnormalities and features of the lung seen on CT imaging, the presence of pulmonary and non-pulmonary comorbid conditions, physiological assessment, and the balance of benefits and risks for the individual patient.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 101
Lung surgical treatments for patients with emphysema
Bullectomy
Giant bullectomy is a rare, but effective procedure for surgical resection of bulla that occupies > one-third of a hemithorax and compresses adjacent viable lung tissue. Reductions in dyspnea, and improvements in lung, respiratory muscle, and cardiac performance, as well as exercise tolerance have been reported.(1196-1198) Blood or thrombin instillation may be effective in those unfit for resection.(1199-1201)
Lung volume reduction surgery (LVRS)
Lung hyperinflation is a major contributor to impaired respiratory function and is associated with increased hospitalization and mortality. Hyperinflation increases the sensation of breathlessness and causes a reduction in exercise due to increased chest wall elastance and reduced respiratory muscle and cardiac mechanics. Hyperinflation is most pronounced in those patients with COPD that have an emphysematous predominant phenotype.
With LVRS, the most emphysematous portions of the lungs are resected to reduce hyperinTflation,(1202) and increase
U lung elastic recoil pressure and density.(1203) The structural changes that result from LVBRS can significantly improve
R expiratory flow and chest wall, respiratory muscle and cardiac mechanics.(1204,1205) that results in improvements in
FEV1, walking distance and quality of life.(1206-1209) LVRS can be performed unilate S rally or bilaterally. In the National
Emphysema Treatment Trial (NETT), a RCT that included severe emphysema pa tients, bilateral LVRS improved survival
R in patients with upper-lobe emphysema and low post-rehabilitation exerciOse capacity.(313) In similar patients with high post-pulmonary rehabilitation exercise capacity, no difference in survivaYl was noted after LVRS, although health status
P and exercise capacity improved. A reinterpretation of the NETT dOata at 5 years post treatment showed sustained
C improvements in lung function, exercise, shortness of breath and quality of life.(1210)
LVRS has been demonstrated to result in higher mortality than medical management in severe emphysema patients
O with FEV1 ≤ 20% predicted and either homogeneous eDmphysema on high resolution computed tomography or a DLco
≤ 20% of predicted.(1211) In addition to a lower DL -co, a lower FEV1 and BMI have also been reported to increase
A mortality.(1212) Postoperative BODE (body mass index, degree of airflow obstruction, level of dyspnea and exercise
R capacity) is a predictor of survival followinEg LVRS.(1213) Successful outcomes with LVRS have been reported in select
T patients with severely impaired DLAco when hyperinflation is severe, and associated with approachable
M emphysematous targets for resection.(1214) Identification of target zones using three-dimensional computed
T tomographic imaging is beneficiHal in selecting resectable target zones.(1215) A prospective economic analysis in NETT
G indicated that LVRS is costly relative to healthcare programs that do not include surgery.(1216)
Post NETT, experienced centers have reported substantial physiological and functional improvements with LVRS with
O reduced morbidityC and mortality.(1217,1218) However, the numbers of patients undergoing LVRS remain low worldwide.(1218,1219) Several patient factors such as difficulty in obtaining referrals, the perception of increased surgical complications, and limited continuity of care are reasons why the numbers of patients undergoing LVRS remain low despite its reported benefits.(1220) Additionally, respiratory physicians are reluctant to refer patients for LVRS because of the uncertainty about the associated complications, or lack of access to a multidisciplinary team to discuss patient candidates.(1221) To achieve successful outcomes, a multidisciplinary team is key to select potential LVRS patients and coordinate postoperative care.(1222)
Lung transplantation
Over 1,000 patients with COPD undergo lung transplantation on an annual basis, about 30.6% of all patients that undergo transplantation.(1223) Since implementation of the lung allocation severity (LAS) scoring system, the numbers of patients undergoing lung transplantation for COPD is exceeded by the numbers of patients receiving transplantation for interstitial lung diseases. Patients with COPD should be referred for consideration of lung transplantation when
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 102 they have progressive disease despite maximal medical treatment, are not candidates for lung volume reduction surgery, have a BODE index of 5 to 6, a PaCO > 50 mmHg (6.6 kPa) and/or PaO < 60 mmHg (8 kPa) and FEV1 < 25%.(1224)
They should be considered for listing for lung transplantation when the BODE index is > 7, FEV1 is < 15% to 20%, and they have had three or more severe exacerbations during the previous year, one severe exacerbation with hypercapnic respiratory failure, or have moderate to severe pulmonary hypertension.(1224) In the last decade, lung transplant has been increasingly performed in patients of older age, higher BMI, prior chest surgery, poor nutritional status, prior evidence of chronic infection, cardiovascular disease, or extrapulmonary comorbid conditions.(1225)
Lung transplantation in patients with COPD has been predominately associated with an improvement in quality of life, not an increase in survival except for COPD patients with severe AATD or those severely impaired with high BODE scores.(1196,1226-1232) The median survival post lung transplantation for COPD is 5.9 years.(1223) Over 70% of lung transplants conducted in COPD patients are double lung transplants; the remainder are single lung transplants.(1233)
Bilateral lung transplantation leads to longer survival in patients with COPD especially in those < 60 years of age.(1234,1235)
Two unique native lung complications have been proposed to account for the Bsuperiority of double lung
R transplantation in patients with COPD, native lung hyperinflation and lung cancer occurrence in the native
T lung.(1236,1237) Lung cancer has been reported to occur in the native lung following S single lung transplantation with an
D incidence of 5.2-6.1%.(1237,1238) Native lung hyperinflation following single lun g transplantation for COPD has been
R reported to occur 15-30% of the time.(1239,1240) Positive pressure ventilatOion in a patient with COPD with an overly compliant native lung coupled with reduced compliance in an edeYmatous allograft may result in native lung
P hyperinflation. However, some studies have shown no impact of sinOgle lung transplant on post-transplant morbidity,
C and even improved survival following single lung transplantatio n in patients with COPD.(1239,1241,1242) Limitations in
T these studies include the retrospective nature of design, tOhe potential for selection bias, missing covariates, and
N center-specific practice patterns.
In general, lung transplantation has limited availab i-lity due to the shortage of donor organs and cost, thus single vs.
A double lung transplantation is balanced between individual patient factors vs. societal demands to increase the donor
R pool for eligible recipients.(1243) The complEications most seen in COPD patients after lung transplantation are acute
T rejection, bronchiolitis obliterans, opportunistic infections and lymphoproliferative disease.(1244)
Bronchoscopic interventioTns in COPD
► In selected patientsR wIith heterogeneous or homogenous emphysema and significant hyperinflation refractory
Y to optimized medical care, surgical or bronchoscopic modes of lung volume reduction (e.g., endobronchial
P one-way valvOes, lung coils or thermal ablation) may be considered.(1245) Some of these therapies (vapor
C ablation and lung coils) are not widely available for clinical care in many countries.
► In selected patients with a large bulla, surgical bullectomy may be considered.
► In selected patients with very severe COPD and without relevant contraindications, lung transplantation may be considered.
Bronchoscopic Interventions to reduce hyperinflation in severe emphysema
Due to the morbidity and mortality associated with LVRS, less invasive bronchoscopic approaches to lung reduction have been examined.(1246) These include a variety of different bronchoscopic procedures to perform lung volume reduction (i.e., endoscopic lung volume reduction, ELVR) including airway bypass stents, endobronchial one-way valves (EBV) , self-activating coils, sealants and thermal ablative techniques.(1246) Although these techniques differ markedly from one another they are similar in their objective to decrease thoracic volume to improve lung, chest wall and respiratory muscle mechanics.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 103
Choosing bronchoscopic lung reduction (endobronchial valve, coil placement or thermal ablation) or surgical resection
(lung volume reduction surgery, LVRS) to treat hyperinflation in an emphysematous patient depends on a number of factors. These include: the extent and pattern of emphysema identified on HRCT; the presence of interlobar collateral ventilation measured by fissure integrity on HRCT or physiological assessment (endoscopic balloon occlusion and flow assessment); regional availability of the various therapies for clinical care, local proficiency in the performance of the procedures; and patient and provider preferences. Bronchoscopic techniques depend upon the presence of an intact fissure between the treated and non-treated lobe for EBV to be successful, but not for the other techniques. Vapor ablation therapy is the only lung reduction therapy that has been reported to be successfully performed at the segmental rather than lobar level.(1247) Figure 3.26 provides an overview of the various interventional and surgical options for patients with emphysema.
Endobronchial one-way valves (EBV)
EBV are the most well studied therapy of all the ELVR techniques. RCTs showed significant increases in FEV1 and 6- minute walk distance as well as health status in subjects selected for the absence of interlobar collateral ventilation compared to the control group at 6 and 12 months.(315,560) Adverse effects in the endobronchial valve treatment group in both studies included pneumothorax, valve removal or valve replacement.(560) Pneumothorax was seen in 26.6% of subjects treated with the endobronchial valve usually within the first 72 hours of the procedure (76%).(315,1248,1249) But benefits have also been shown in patients with heterogeneous compared to those with homogenous emphysema in one study.(560)
Early-onset pneumothorax in the EBV treated group likely results from lung structural changes due to acute volume
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 104 reduction in the emphysematous targeted lobe by valve therapy that triggers rapid ipsilateral non-targeted lobe expansion, a recognized indicator of successful target lobe occlusion in patients with intact fissures or absence of collateral ventilation.(314) Pleural adhesions may also be a contributing factor to the development of a pneumothorax.(1250) The occurrence of pneumothorax highlights the need for physicians performing this procedure to have expertise in the management of procedural complications.(314)
After the post-procedural period however, patients treated with EBV compared to usual care tend to have a lower number of exacerbations and episodes of respiratory failure. A comparison of treatment benefits and complications associated with EBV compared to LVRS show comparable benefits with endobronchial valve treatment but with fewer complications.(315) Additionally, ELVR has similar beneficial effects whether it is performed in the upper or lower lobes.(314,315)
Improved survival has been associated with post procedural atelectasis of the treated lobe post EBV.(1251-1253) Improved survival has also been reported in patients with severe hyperinflation undergoing EBV compared to a matched
E population not undergoing ELVR.(1254) T
When preferences for medical treatment for patients with severe emphysema are elicited, the majority chose
T treatments with EBV over LVRS or continued medial therapy.(1255) ELVR with EBV S is clinically available and approved
D for treatment in many countries in the treatment of patients who ha ve intact fissures or lack collateral
R ventilation.(315,1256,1257) O
The following bronchoscopic lung volume reduction techniques do nOot depend upon the presence of intact fissures or
C absence of collateral ventilation.
Airway bypass stents
Airway bypass stents are transbronchial passages thaDt are created through the walls of the central airways into the
- emphysematous parenchyma to facilitate the empt ying of trapped gas. In a prospective randomized controlled clinical
A trial, patients had short term improvements, but no durable improvements were found in lung function, 6 MWD or
R quality of life.(1258) E
Sealants
A multicenter study examining tHhe effects of a lung sealant to create lung reduction was discontinued prematurely;
G while the study reported significant benefits in some physiologic parameters, the intervention was associated with
R significant morbidity and Ymortality.(1259)
Vapor ablation
In a prospective RCT, targeted thermal vapour ablation of more diseased emphysematous segments to produce fibrosis and atelectasis resulted in clinically meaningful and statistically significant improvements in lung function and health status at 6 months. COPD exacerbation was the most common serious adverse event. Durability of these changes was subsequently reported at 12 months follow-up.(1247,1260) This therapy has limited clinical availability.
Self-activating coils
Multicenter trials have examined nitinol coils implanted into the lung compared to usual care on changes in 6-minute walk distance, lung function and health status in patients with advanced homogenous and heterogeneous emphysema. Studies reported an increase in 6-minute walk distance with coil treatment compared to control and smaller improvements in FEV1, and quality of life measured by St George’s Respiratory Questionnaire.(1261-1263) Patients with baseline residual volume > 200% predicted, emphysema score > 20% low attenuation area, and absence of airway disease are more likely to have clinically meaningful improvements in lung function and quality of life.(1264)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 105
Major complications included pneumonia, pneumothorax, hemoptysis and COPD exacerbations occurring more frequently in the coil group.(1262) This therapy has limited clinical availability.
Additional data are needed to define the optimal bronchoscopic lung volume technique to produce bronchoscopic lung volume reduction in patients who lack fissure integrity, or exhibit collateral ventilation, and to refine the procedure to reduce complications and improve longer term clinical outcomes.(1262)
Sequential performance of LVRS or ELVR prior to or following lung transplantation
Because COPD is a progressive disease, LVRS or ELVR may be followed by lung transplantation. Conversely, patients who undergo single lung transplantation may subsequently undergo LVRS or ELVR to treat the hyperinflated native lung. In hyperinflated patients with advanced emphysema, LVRS or ELVR might be effective treatments to either delay the need for lung transplantation or optimize the condition of patients who may eventually require lung transplantation.(1265-1267) In some patients following single lung transplantation, the performaEnce of LVRS or ELVR to
T decrease native lung hyperinflation may improve lung function and performance statusU.(1268-1273) The incidence of
B postoperative bleeding requiring re-exploration and renal dysfunction requiring dialysis or the use of extracorporeal
R membrane oxygenation (ECMO) may be higher in patients undergoing lung transpTlantation following LVRS.(1274,1275)
Previous ELVR has been reported to have no impact on morbidity or survival post sIubsequent lung transplantation but
D may affect microbial colonization.(1275,1276)
Airway predominant treatments
Abnormalities that predominantly involve the airways, such as excessive dynamic collapse of the large airways
(tracheobronchomalacia) chronic bronchitis and frequent and sTev ere exacerbations not responsive to optimal medical
O treatment pose significant clinical challenges. N
Excessive dynamic airway collapse (EDAC)
 EDAC or tracheobronchomalacia (TBM) is a disorLder of the large airways where abnormal collapsibility occurs with
A expiration. Commons symptoms are dyspneRa, Icough and wheezing with inability to expectorate phlegm. In a cross E sectional analysis of smokers, the presence of excessive dynamic airway collapse observed on CT imaging was 5% and
A associated with worsened quality of life and more frequent and severe exacerbations.(1277) Airway stenting and
M tracheoplasty may be beneficial in se lect patients.(1278,1279)
Chronic bronchitis is a commRoIn and significant contributor to a worsening of patient’s symptoms of cough and sputum
Y production and cause worsened quality of life and increased mortality. No specific medical intervention significantly
P and consistently allOeviates chronic bronchitis. Newer interventions have been proposed to reduce mucus
C hypersecretion by eliminating airway goblet cell hyperplasia and submucosal glands.
Nitrogen cryospray
Liquid nitrogen metered cryospray is delivered to the central airways and ablates the epithelium to a depth of 0.1 to
0.5 mm.(1108) After treatment, rapid regeneration of normal epithelium occurs without scarring and may potentially treat chronic bronchitis.(1280)
Another novel treatment for chronic bronchitis is rheoplasty.(1281) Rheoplasty delivers short bursts of high frequency electrical energy to the airway epithelium targeting submucosal tissues and goblet cells to facilitate their replacement with healthier tissue. Ongoing phase III randomized clinical trials are evaluating the efficacy of these therapies.(1282,1283)
Lung denervation
Targeted lung denervation is another therapy currently undergoing phase III clinical trial study to determine its impact
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 106 of frequent moderate or severe exacerbations in patients with COPD already on maximal inhaled respiratory treatment.(1284,1285) The therapy intends to disrupt the parasympathetic nerve transmission to and from the lungs. In patients with COPD, basal parasympathetic tone is elevated and increases acetylcholine levels and mucus production and airway contraction. The treatment uses a water-cooled catheter with radiofrequency energy to disrupt parasympathetic nerve transmission while protecting the airway surface.(1110,1111,1285,1286)
Key points for interventional therapy in stable COPD are summarized in Figure 3.27.
Surgery in the COPD patient
General surgery
Postoperative pulmonary complications are as important and common as postoperative cardiac complications and, consequently, are a key component of the increased risk posed by general surgery in COPD patients.(1287) The key factors that can contribute to the risk include smoking, poor general health status, age, obesity, and COPD severity. A comprehensive definition of postoperative pulmonary complications should include only major pulmonary respiratory complications, namely lung infections, atelectasis and/or increased airflow obstruction, which all potentially result in
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 107 acute respiratory failure and aggravation of COPD.(1288-1290)
Increased risk of postoperative pulmonary complications in COPD patients may vary with the severity of COPD, although the surgical site is the most important predictor and risk increases as the incision approaches the diaphragm.(1290) Most reports conclude that epidural or spinal anesthesia have a lower risk than general anesthesia, although the results are not totally uniform. Some studies conducted in patients undergoing sham bronchoscopic procedures have reported acute exacerbation rates as high as 8.4%.(1258) These data suggest that intubation and/or simple airway manipulation may increase the risk of exacerbation in select COPD patients.
To prevent postoperative pulmonary complications, stable COPD patients clinically symptomatic and/or with limited exercise capacity should be treated medically intensively before surgery, with all the measures already well established for stable COPD patients who are not about to have surgery. The presence of comorbid conditions, especially cardiac abnormalities, should be systemically assessed and treated before any major surgical intervention.
Lung resection T
For lung resection, the individual patient’s risk factors should be identified by careful history taking including physical
R examination, chest radiography, and pulmonary function tests. Although the vaTlue of pulmonary function tests
S remains contentious, there is consensus that all COPD candidates for lung resIection should undergo a complete
D battery of tests, including spirometry with bronchodilator response, static lung v olumes, diffusing capacity, and arterial
R blood gases at rest.(1291,1292) COPD patients at high risk for surgical compOlications due to poor lung function should
Y undergo further assessment, for example, tests of regional distribution of perfusion and exercise capacity.(1291,1292)
The risk of postoperative complications from lung resection a ppears to be increased in patients with decreased
O predicted postoperative pulmonary function (FEV1 or DLco < 30-40% predicted) or exercise capacity (peak VO < 10
N 2 ml/kg/min or 35% predicted). The final decision to pursOue surgery should be made after discussion with the surgeon, pulmonary specialist, primary clinician, and the patienDt. Surgery should be postponed if an exacerbation is present.

CHAPTER 4: MANAGEMENT OF EXACERBATIONS
KEY POINTS:
• An exacerbation of COPD is defined as an event characterized by dyspnea and/or cough and sputum that worsen over < 14 days. Exacerbations of COPD are often associated with increased local and systemic inflammation caused by airway infection, pollution, or other insults to the lungs.
• As the symptoms are not specific to COPD relevant differential diagnoses should be considered, particularly pneumonia, congestive heart failure and pulmonary embolism.
• The goals for treatment of COPD exacerbations are to minimize the negative impact of the current exacerbation and to prevent subsequent events. E
• Short-acting inhaled beta -agonists, with or without short-acting antBicholinergics, are
I recommended as the initial bronchodilators to treat an exacerbationR.
• Maintenance therapy with long-acting bronchodilators shouDldI be initiated as soon as possible. In patients with frequent exacerbations and eleRvated blood eosinophil levels
O addition of inhaled corticosteroids to the double bronchodilator regimen should be
Y considered.
• In patients with severe exacerbations, systemic corticosteroids can improve lung function
(FEV1), oxygenation and shorten recovery time including hospitalization duration.
Duration of therapy should not normally bNe more than 5 days.
• Antibiotics, when indicated, can shorten recovery time, reduce the risk of early relapse,
- treatment failure, and hospitalizLat ion duration. Duration of therapy should be 5 days.
• Methylxanthines are not recommended due to increased side effect profiles.
• Non-invasive mechanical ventilation should be the first mode of ventilation used in COPD
M patients with acute respiratory failure who have no absolute contraindication because it
T improves gas exHchange, reduces work of breathing and the need for intubation, decreases
G hospitalization duration and improves survival.
• ExacerPbation recovery time varies, taking up to 4-6 weeks to recover, with some patients
O failing to return to the pre-exacerbation functional state. Following an exacerbation,
C appropriate measures for exacerbation prevention should be initiated (see Chapter 3).
DEFINITION
An exacerbation of chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (ECOPD) is defined as an event characterized by increased dyspnea and/or cough and sputum that worsens in < 14 days which may be accompanied by tachypnea and/or tachycardia and is often associated with increased local and systemic inflammation caused by infection, pollution, or other insult to the airways.(328)
Considerations
Exacerbations of COPD are important events in the management of COPD because they negatively impact health
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 109 status, rates of hospitalization and readmission, and disease progression.(483,484) COPD exacerbations are usually associated with increased airway inflammation, increased mucus production and marked gas trapping. These changes contribute to increased dyspnea that is the key symptom of an exacerbation. Other symptoms include increased sputum purulence and volume, together with increased cough and wheeze.(1293,1294) Patients with COPD are at increased risk of other acute events, particularly decompensated heart failure,(1295,1296) pneumonia,(1297,1298) pulmonary embolism(1299,1300) that may also mimic or aggravate an ECOPD. Thus, while worsening of dyspnea, particularly if associated with cough and, purulent sputum, and no other symptoms or signs in a patient with COPD may be diagnosed as an ECOPD, other patients may have worsening of respiratory symptoms, particularly dyspnea without the classic characteristics of ECOPD, that should prompt careful consideration and/or search of those potential confounders, or contributors. In some patients one or more of these diagnoses may contribute to the clinical presentations and should be addressed appropriately (Figure 4.1).
Currently, exacerbations are classified after the event has occurred as:
► Mild (treated with short acting bronchodilators only, SABDs)
► Moderate (treated with SABDs and oral corticosteroids ± antibiotics) or
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 110
► Severe (patient requires hospitalization or visits the emergency room). Severe exacerbations may also be associated with acute respiratory failure.
The current grading of the severity of an ECOPD, based on post facto use of healthcare resources, is a major limitation of the current definition. Because of global variability in the available resources to treat patients and local customs affecting the criteria for hospital visits and admissions, there is substantial variability in reported ECOPD outcomes.(1301)
Figure 4.2 shows a proposed clinical approach based on the current best available evidence.(328)
It has been proposed that these easy to obtain clinical variables can help define the severity of exacerbations on point
O of contact (Rome cClassification).(328) Using the Rome classification for exacerbations, hospitalized patients with acute exacerbations can be further subclassified into mild, moderate and severe events with differences in mortality.(1302,1303)
Based on a thorough review of the available literature and using a Delphi approach to agree on the variable thresholds, the severity classification is summarized in Figure 4.3. The severity of exacerbations assessed using this grading scheme correlates with prognosis.
In the primary care setting, where laboratories may not be available, severity can be determined with the easily obtainable dyspnea intensity (using a VAS 0 to 10 dyspnea scale with zero being not short of breath at all and 10 the worst shortness of breath you have ever experienced), respiratory rate, heart rate and oxygen saturation level. Where available, blood C-reactive protein (CRP) level is recommended. To determine the need for ventilator support (usually in the emergency room or hospital setting) arterial blood gases or equivalent should be measured. To move from a mild to a moderate level, three of the variables need to exceed the established thresholds. It is hoped that prospective validation will help better define exacerbations and their severity at point of contact, and that documented validation
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 111 may confirm or help modify the proposed thresholds of the variables now included. It is proposed that prospective research can help determine a more specific marker of lung injury than the more generic CRP, as has been true for other organs acute events.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 112
It is now recognized that many exacerbations are not reported to healthcare professionals for therapy and yet these events, although often shorter in duration, also have a significant impact on health status.(1304,1305) Thus, COPD patients need to receive education about the importance of understanding exacerbation symptoms and when to seek professional healthcare. The WHO has defined a minimum set of interventions for the management of exacerbations.(891)
Exacerbations are mainly triggered by respiratory viral infections although bacterial infections and environmental factors such as ambient air pollution and excess heat may also initiate and/or amplify these events.(1306,1307) Short-term exposure to fine (PM2.5) and coarse (PM10) particulate matter is associated with increased hospitalizations, ER visits, and outpatient visits,(1307) as well as increased mortality of COPD exacerbations.(1306,1308,1309) Another study also showed that short-term exposure to ambient nitrogen dioxide and PM2.5 was associated with exacerbations in mild to moderate COPD patients.(105) The most common viruses isolated are human rhinovirus (the cause of the common cold), influenza, para-influenza and metapneumovirus which can be detected for up to a week after an exacerbation onset.(1310,1311) When associated with viral infections, exacerbations are often more severe, last longer and precipitate
E more hospitalizations, as seen during winter. Filamentous fungi, particularly Aspergillus specTies, may be identified in
U sputum samples of patients during moderate or severe exacerbations(1312-1314) although thBeir clinical relevance remains
R unclear. Invasive pulmonary aspergillosis is rare (1.3%-3.9%)(1315) and more frequent in patients with more severe
S baseline airflow obstruction, recent use of broad-spectrum antibiotics or parenteral steroids, and
D hypoalbuminemia.(1316) Aspergillus sensitization is also a marker of increased ri sk of exacerbations.(1317) The diagnostic
R approach to invasive aspergillosis in this setting remains challenging.(1318) O
Exacerbations can be associated with increased sputum productiOon and, if purulent, they are most likely due to
C bacterial infection(1294,1310,1319) There is reasonable evidence to su pport the concept that eosinophils are increased in
T the airways, lung, and blood in a significant proportion oOf people with COPD.(1320-1322) The presence of sputum
N eosinophilia has been related to susceptibility to viral infection.(1319) It has been suggested that exacerbations
O associated with an increase in sputum or blood eosinoDphils may be more responsive to systemic steroids(1323) although more prospective trials are needed to test this hypo -thesis.(1323)
During a COPD exacerbation, increased syEmptoms are usually present for 7 to 10 days, but some events may last
T longer. At 8 weeks up to 20% of patients will not have recovered to their pre-exacerbation state.(1324) COPD
M exacerbations contribute to disease progression,(1325) which is more likely if recovery from exacerbations is slow.(1326)
Exacerbations can also cluster inH time and once they occur there is increased likelihood of another event(487,1327) (see
Chapter 2).
Some patients are susceptible to frequent exacerbations (defined as two or more exacerbations per year), and these
O patients have worsCe health status and morbidity than patients with less frequent exacerbations.(484) The exact reason for an individual’s increased susceptibility to exacerbation symptoms remains largely unknown. However, the perception of breathlessness is greater in frequent exacerbators than infrequent exacerbators,(549) suggesting that a perception of breathing difficulty may contribute to precipitating the respiratory symptoms rather than solely physiological, or causative factors. The strongest predictor of a patient’s future exacerbation frequency remains the number of exacerbations they have had in the prior year.(487) It is recognized that these patients form a moderately stable phenotype, although some studies have shown that a significant proportion of patients change their exacerbation frequency especially with worsening FEV1.(1328)
Other factors that have been associated with an increased risk of acute exacerbations and/or severity of exacerbations include an increase in the ratio of the pulmonary artery to aorta cross sectional dimension (i.e., ratio > 1),(325) a greater percentage of emphysema or airway wall thickness(563) measured by chest CT imaging and the presence of chronic bronchitis.(183,1329)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 113
Vitamin D has an immune-modulating role and has been implicated in the pathophysiology of exacerbations. As with many chronic diseases vitamin D levels are lower in people with COPD than in healthy individuals. Some, but not all studies have shown that supplementation in people with severe deficiency results in a 50% reduction in episodes and hospital admission.(1049,1330) Therefore, it is recommended that all patients hospitalized for exacerbations should be assessed and investigated for severe deficiency (< 10 ng/ml or < 25 nM) followed by supplementation if required.
TREATMENT OPTIONS
Treatment setting
The goals of treatment for COPD exacerbations are to minimize the negative impact of the current exacerbation and prevent the development of subsequent events.(1331) Depending on the severity of an exacerbation and/or the severity of the underlying disease, an exacerbation can be managed in either the outpatient or inpatiEent setting. More than
80% of exacerbations are managed on an outpatient basis with pharmacological therapiesU including bronchodilators,
B corticosteroids, and antibiotics.(487,889,1332)
The indications for assessing the need for hospitalization during a COPD exacerbation are shown in Figure 4.4. When
C patients with a COPD exacerbation come to the emergency department, if hypoxemic they should be provided with supplemental oxygen and undergo assessment to determine whether the exacerbation is life-threatening and if increased work of breathing or impaired gas exchange requires consideration for non-invasive ventilation. If so, healthcare providers should consider admission to an area where proper monitoring and care can be provided. In less severe cases, the patient may be managed in the emergency department or hospital ward unit. In addition to pharmacological therapy, hospital management of exacerbations includes respiratory support (oxygen therapy, ventilation). The management of severe, but not life threatening, exacerbations is outlined in Figure 4.5.
The clinical presentation of COPD exacerbation is heterogeneous; thus, we recommend that in hospitalized patients the severity of the exacerbation should be based on the patient’s clinical signs and recommend the following classification:(1333)

Venturi mask 24-35% inspired oxygen (FiO ); no increase in PaCO .
