> 35% FiO ; hypercarbia i.e., PaCO increased compared with baseline or elevated 50-60 mmHg.
Acute respiratory failure – life-threatening: Respiratory rate: > 24 breaths per minute; using accessory respiratory muscles; acute changes in mental status; hypoxemia not improved with supplemental oxygen via Venturi mask or requiring FiO > 40%; hypercarbia i.e., PaCO increased compared with baseline or elevated > 60 mmHg or the
2 2 presence of acidosis (pH ≤ 7.25).
Long-term prognosis following hospitalization for COPD exacerbation is poor, with a five-year mortality rate of about
50%.(1334) Factors independently associated with poor outcome include older age, lower BMI, comorbidities (e.g., cardiovascular disease or lung cancer), previous hospitalizations for COPD exacerbations, clinical severity of the index exacerbation and need for long-term oxygen therapy at discharge.(1335-1337) Patients characterized by a higher prevalence and severity of respiratory symptoms, poorer quality of life, worse lung function, lower exercise capacity, lower lung density and thickened bronchial walls on CT-scan are also at increased risk for a higher mortality following
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 115 an acute COPD exacerbation.(1338) COPD exacerbations increase the risk of adverse cardiovascular events, with heightened risk in the first 30 days persisting for up to 1-2 years.(1339) Mortality risk may be heightened during spells of cold weather.(1340)
An updated Cochrane review concluded that the use of COPD exacerbation action plans with a single short educational component, in conjunction with ongoing support, reduced in-hospital healthcare utilization. Such educational interventions were also found to increase the treatment of COPD exacerbations with corticosteroids and antibiotics.(1341)
Key points for the management of all exacerbations are given in Figure 4.6.
Pharmacological treatment
The three classes of medicatioGns most commonly used for COPD exacerbations are bronchodilators, corticosteroids,
R and antibiotics.
BronchodilatorsC
Although there is no high-quality evidence from RCTs, it is recommended that short-acting inhaled beta -agonists, with or without short-acting anticholinergics, are the initial bronchodilators for acute treatment of a COPD exacerbation.(1288,1342) A systematic review of the route of delivery of short-acting bronchodilators found no significant differences in FEV1 between using metered dose inhalers (MDI) (with or without a spacer device) or nebulizers to deliver the agent,(592,1343) although the latter may be an easier delivery method for sicker patients. It is recommended that patients do not receive continuous nebulization but use the MDI inhaler one or two puffs every one hour for two or three doses and then every 2-4 hours based on the patient’s response. Although, there are no clinical studies that have evaluated the use of inhaled long-acting bronchodilators (either beta -agonists or anticholinergics or combinations) with or without ICS during an exacerbation, we recommend continuing these treatments during the exacerbation or to start these medications as soon as possible before hospital discharge. Intravenous methylxanthines
(theophylline or aminophylline) are not recommended to use in these patients due to significant side effects.(1344,1345)
If a nebulizer is chosen to deliver the bronchodilator agent, air-driven bronchodilator nebulization is preferable to oxygen-driven in acute exacerbations of COPD in order to avoid the potential risk of increasing the PaCO associated
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 116 with oxygen-driven bronchodilator administration.(1346)
Glucocorticoids
Data from studies (mostly hospital based) indicate that systemic glucocorticoids in COPD exacerbations shorten recovery time and improve lung function (FEV1). They also improve oxygenation,(1347-1350) the risk of early relapse, treatment failure,(1351) and the length of hospitalization.(1347,1349,1352) A dose of 40 mg prednisone-equivalent per day for 5 days is recommended.(1353) One observational study suggests that longer courses of oral corticosteroids for COPD exacerbations are associated with an increased risk of pneumonia and mortality.(1354) Therapy with oral prednisolone is equally effective to intravenous administration.(1355) Nebulized budesonide alone may be a suitable alternative for treatment of exacerbations in some patients,(1348,1356,1357) and provides similar benefits to intravenous methylprednisolone, although the choice between these options may depend on local cost issues.(1358,1359) Even short bursts of corticosteroids are associated with subsequent increased risk of pneumonia, sepsis and death(1360) and use should be confined to patients with significant exacerbations. Recent studies suggest that glucocorticoids may be less efficacious to treat acute COPD exacerbations in patients with lower levels of blood eosinopEhils(487,1320,1323,1361) and
T more trials of steroid-sparing treatment regimens are required. A double-blind, placebo-cUontrolled RCT (STARR2) of
B prednisolone therapy guided by blood eosinophil count during acute COPD exacerbation found that this strategy was
R non-inferior to standard care including systematic prednisolone treatment (30 mg pTer day for 14 days) and allowed a
33% reduction in the proportion of patients receiving prednisolone.(1362) The sItudy authors concluded that blood
D eosinophil-directed prednisolone can be used to safely reduce systemic glucoc orticoid use in clinical practice.(1362)
Antibiotics
Although the infectious agents in COPD exacerbations can be viral or bacterial,(1311,1363) the use of antibiotics in
C exacerbations remains controversial.(352,1364,1365) The uncertainTti es originate from studies that did not differentiate
O between bronchitis (acute or chronic) and COPD exacerNbations, studies without placebo-control, and/or studies without chest X-rays that do not exclude that patienOts may have had underlying pneumonia. There is evidence
D supporting the use of antibiotics in exacerbations when patients have clinical signs of a bacterial infection e.g.,
- increased sputum purulence.(352,1365) Indeed, the uLse of observed sputum color can safely modulate antibiotic therapy
A with no adverse effects if sputum is white or RclIear in color. On the other hand, observed sputum purulence has 94.4% sensitivity and 52% specificity for high bact E erial load, indicative of a causative relationship.(352)
A systematic review of placebo-con trolled studies has shown that antibiotics reduce the risk of short-term mortality
T by 77%, treatment failure by H 53% and sputum purulence by 44%.(1366) The review provides evidence to treat
G moderately or severely ill pIatients with COPD exacerbations and increased cough and sputum purulence with
R antibiotics.(1366,1367) TheseY data are supported by more RCTs in patients with diagnoses of moderate COPD.(1368) In an
RCT, the addition oOf doxycycline to oral corticosteroid an outpatient setting did not prolong time to next
C exacerbation.(1369) In the outpatient setting, sputum cultures are not feasible as they take at least two days and frequently do not give reliable results for technical reasons. Several biomarkers of airway infection are being studied in exacerbations of COPD that have a better diagnostic profile. Earlier studies of C-reactive protein (CRP) have reported contradictory findings.(1370,1371) A randomized trial found a marked reduction in antibiotic prescriptions without impaired outcomes in UK primary care outpatients with ECOPD in whom antibiotics prescriptions were guided by point-of-care CRP testing.(1372) Another trial in patients hospitalized for exacerbations of COPD in The Netherlands found similar results (reduced antibiotic use with no increase in treatment failure). These findings need confirmation in other settings before a recommendation to generalize this approach. However, data has indicated that antibiotic usage can be safely reduced from 77.4% to 47.7% when CRP is low.(1373)
Procalcitonin is an acute phase reactant that increases in response to inflammation and infection and has been studied to determine the use of antibiotics in COPD exacerbations.(1374) The efficacy of this biomarker is controversial. Several studies, mainly done in the outpatient setting, suggested that procalcitonin-guided antibiotic treatment reduces
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 117 antibiotic exposure and side effects with the same clinical efficacy.(1375-1377) A systematic review and meta-analysis on the use of procalcitonin in hospitalized patients with a COPD exacerbation found no significant reduction in overall antibiotic exposure.(1378) In patients with COPD exacerbations treated in an ICU setting, the use of a procalcitonin- based algorithm for initiating or stopping antibiotics was associated with a higher mortality rate when compared to those receiving standard antibiotic regimens.(1379) Based on these conflicting results we cannot recommend at this time the use of procalcitonin-based protocols to make the decision on using antibiotics in patient with COPD exacerbations; however, confirmatory trials with rigorous methodology are required.
In summary, antibiotics should be given to patients with exacerbations of COPD who have three cardinal symptoms: increase in dyspnea, sputum volume, and sputum purulence; have two of the cardinal symptoms, if increased purulence of sputum is one of the two symptoms; or require mechanical ventilation (invasive or noninvasive).(1294,1311)
A metanalysis demonstrated that ≤ 5 days of antibiotic treatment had the same clinical and bacteriological efficacy to longer conventional treatment in outpatients with COPD exacerbations. Furthermore, shorter exposure to antibiotics may decrease the risk developing antimicrobial resistance and complications associated with this therapy. The
E recommended length of antibiotic therapy is 5-7 days.(1380) We recommend a duration oTf ≤ 5 days of antibiotic
U treatment for outpatient treatment of COPD exacerbations.(1379,1381) B
The choice of the antibiotic should be based on the local bacterial resistance pattern. Usually, initial empirical
D treatment is an aminopenicillin with clavulanic acid, macrolide, tetracycline or, in selected patients, quinolone. In
R patients with frequent exacerbations, severe airflow obstruction,(1382,1383) Oand/or exacerbations requiring mechanical ventilation,(1384) cultures from sputum or other materials from the luYng should be performed, as gram-negative
P bacteria (e.g., Pseudomonas species) or resistant pathogens that areO not sensitive to the above-mentioned antibiotics
C may be present. The route of administration (oral or intraveno us) depends on the patient’s ability to eat and the
T pharmacokinetics of the antibiotic, although it is preferable tOhat antibiotics be given orally. Improvements in dyspnea
N and sputum purulence suggest clinical success.
 Adjunct therapies
Depending on the clinical condition of the pIatient, an appropriate fluid balance, use of diuretics when clinically
R indicated, anticoagulants, treatment of coEmorbidities and nutritional aspects should be considered. Among COPD
T patients hospitalized with a suspectedA exacerbation, up to 5.9% were found to have pulmonary embolism.(1299)
Hospitalized patients with COPD are at an increased risk of deep vein thrombosis and pulmonary embolism(1385,1386)
T and prophylactic measures for Hthromboembolism should be instituted.(1387,1388) At all times, healthcare providers
G should strongly enforce the need for smoking cessation.
Respiratory support
Oxygen therapy
This is a key component of hospital treatment of an exacerbation. Supplemental oxygen should be titrated to improve the patient’s hypoxemia with a target saturation of 88-92%.(1389) Once oxygen is started, blood gases should be checked frequently, or as clinically indicated, to ensure satisfactory oxygenation without carbon dioxide retention and/or worsening acidosis. Pulse oximetry is not as accurate as arterial blood gas(548) and in particular, may overestimate blood oxygen content among individuals with darker skin tones.(1390) A study demonstrated that venous blood gas to assess bicarbonate levels and pH is accurate when compared with arterial blood gas assessment.(1391) Additional data are needed to clarify the utility of venous blood gas sampling to make clinical decisions in scenarios of acute respiratory failure; most patients included had a pH > 7.30 on presentation, PCO levels were dissimilar when measured by venous compared to arterial blood samples and the severity of airflow obstruction was not reported.(1391) Venturi masks offer more accurate and controlled delivery of oxygen than do nasal prongs.(1288)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 118
High-flow nasal therapy
High-flow nasal therapy (HFNT) delivers heated and humidified air-oxygen blends via special devices (e.g.,
Vapotherm®, Comfort Flo®, or Optiflow®) at rates up to 8 L/min in infants and up to 60 L/min in adults.(1392) HFNT has been associated with decreased respiratory rate and effort, decreased work of breathing, improved gas exchange, improved lung volume and dynamic compliance, transpulmonary pressures and homogeneity.(1393,1394) These physiologic benefits positively improve oxygenation and clinical outcomes in patients with acute hypoxemic respiratory failure.(1393-1396) HFNT has been reported to improve oxygenation and ventilation, decrease hypercarbia and improve health-related quality of life in patients with acute hypercapnia during an acute exacerbation, and also in select patients with stable hypercapnic COPD.(1393,1397-1399) However, the small sample sizes, heterogeneity of the patient populations and short duration of follow-up are current limitations in the interpretation of the value of HFNT for the COPD patient population at large.(1400) HFNT has been reported to improve oxygenation and ventilation, decrease hypercarbia, prolong the time to next moderate exacerbation and improve health-related quality of life scores in patients with acute hypercapnia during an exacerbation or in select patients with stable hypercapnic COPD receiving long term oxygen therapy.(1401) HFNT did not prevent intubation in a RCT conducted Ein patients hospitalized
T with an acute exacerbation.(1402) It should be noted that European Respiratory Society (ERS)U Clinical Practice Guidelines
B recommend trialling NIV prior to use of HFNT in patients with COPD and hypercapnic ARF.(1403) There is a need for well I
R designed, prospective, randomized and controlled multicenter trials to study the effeTcts of HFNT in people with COPD
S experiencing episodes of either acute or chronic hypercapnic respiratory failure. I
Ventilatory support
Some patients need immediate admission to the respiratory care or iPntensive care unit (ICU) (Figure 4.7). Admission
O of patients with severe exacerbations to intermediate or special respiratory care units may be appropriate if adequate
C personnel skills and equipment exist to identify and manageT acute respiratory failure. Ventilatory support in an
O exacerbation can be provided by either noninvasive (nNasal or facial mask) or invasive (oro-tracheal tube or tracheostomy) ventilation. Respiratory stimulants are noOt recommended for acute respiratory failure.(1342)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 119
Noninvasive mechanical veHntilation
The use of noninvasive mechIanical ventilation (NIV) is preferred over invasive ventilation (intubation and positive
R pressure ventilation) as tYhe initial mode of ventilation to treat acute respiratory failure in patients hospitalized for
P acute exacerbations oOf COPD. NIV has been studied in RCTs showing a success rate of 80-85%.(783,1404-1407) NIV has been
C shown to improve oxygenation and acute respiratory acidosis i.e., NIV increases pH and decreases PaCO . NIV also decreases respiratory rate, work of breathing and the severity of breathlessness but also decreases complications such as ventilator associated pneumonia, and length of hospital stay. More importantly, mortality and intubation rates are reduced by this intervention.(1405,1408-1410) Once patients improve and can tolerate at least 4 hours of unassisted breathing, NIV can be directly discontinued without any need for a “weaning” period.(1411) The indications for NIV(1407) are summarized in Figure 4.8.
Invasive mechanical ventilation
The indications for initiating invasive mechanical ventilation during an exacerbation are shown in Figure 4.9, and include failure of an initial trial of NIV.(1412) As experience is gained with the generalized clinical use of NIV in COPD, a number of indications for invasive mechanical ventilation are being successfully treated with NIV, thus eliminating invasive mechanical ventilation as first line treatment of acute respiratory failure during hospitalization for COPD exacerbation.(1412) In patients who fail non-invasive ventilation as initial therapy and receive invasive ventilation as
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 120 subsequent rescue therapy, morbidity, hospital length of stay and mortality are greater.(783) The use of invasive ventilation in patients with very severe COPD is influenced by the likely reversibility of the precipitating event, the patient’s wishes, and the availability of intensive care facilities.(783) When possible, a clear statement of the patient’s own treatment wishes, such as an advance directive or “living will”, makes these difficult decisions easier to resolve.
Major hazards include the risk of ventilator-acquired pneumonia (especially when multi-resistant organisms are prevalent), barotrauma and volutrauma, and the risk of tracheostomy and consequential prolonged ventilation.
Acute mortality among COPD patients with respiratory failure is lower than mortality among patients ventilated for non-COPD causes.(1413) Despite this, there is evidence that patients who might otherwise survive are frequently denied admission to intensive care for intubation because of unwarranted prognostic pessimism.(1414) A large study of COPD patients with acute respiratory failure reported in-hospital mortality of 17-49%.(1415) Further deaths were reported over the next 12 months, particularly among those patients who had poor lung function before invasive ventilation
(FEV1 < 30% predicted), had a non-respiratory comorbidity, or were housebound. Patients who did not have a previously diagnosed comorbidity, had respiratory failure due to a potentially reversible cause (such as an infection),
E or were relatively mobile and not using long-term oxygen, did well after ventilator support. T
Hospital discharge and follow-up R
The cause, severity, impact, treatment and time course of exacerbations varies fDroIm patient to patient and facilities in the community, and healthcare systems, differ from country to country. AccRordingly, there are no standards that can
O be applied to the timing and nature of discharge. However, it is recogniz ed that recurrent exacerbations leading to
Y short-term readmission and increased all-cause mortality are associaPted with the initial hospitalization for an acute
O episode of deterioration.(1416) C
When features related to re-hospitalization and mortNality have been studied, defects in perceived optimal management have been identified including spirometricO assessment and arterial blood gas analysis.(1417) A systematic
D review has shown that comorbidities, previous exace rbations and hospitalization, and increased length of stay were
- significant risk factors for 30- and 90-day all-causeL readmission after an index hospitalization with an exacerbation of
COPD.(1418) Mortality relates to patient age,R tIhe presence of acidotic respiratory failure, the need for ventilatory
E support and comorbidities including anxiety and depression.(1419)
The introduction of care bundles at hospital discharge to include education, optimization of medication, supervision
H and correction of inhaler technique, assessment and optimal management of comorbidities, early rehabilitation,
G telemonitoring and continuReId patient contact have all been investigated to address these issues (Figure 4.10).(1420)
While these measures all seem sensible there is insufficient data that they influence either readmission rates or short P term mortality(1417,14O19,1421,1422) and there is little evidence of cost-effectiveness.(1419) One RCT showed that
C telemonitoring did not change hospitalization or exacerbation rates in people with COPD.(1423) Nevertheless, it remains good clinical practice to cover these issues before discharge and their effectiveness on health status and readmission rates may be increased if they are delivered with an approach that includes motivational interview-based health coaching.(1117)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 121
The only possible exception is early rehabTilitation as there is some evidence that this factor is associated with increased
A mortality, although the reasons remMain unknown.(1422) However, other data suggest that early rehabilitation post hospital discharge (i.e., < 4 weeks)T m ay be associated with improved survival.(849)
Early follow-up (within oneR month) following discharge should be undertaken when possible and has been related to
Y less exacerbation-relatePd readmissions.(1424) There are many patient issues that prevent early follow-up; those not
O attending early follow-up have increased 90-day mortality. This may reflect both patient compliance, limited access to
C medical care, poor social support, and/or the presence of more severe disease. Nevertheless, early follow-up permits a careful review of discharge therapy and an opportunity to make any needed changes in therapy.
Additional follow-up at three months is recommended to ensure return to a stable clinical state and permit a review of the patient’s symptoms, lung function (by spirometry), and where possible the assessment of prognosis using multiple scoring systems such as BODE.(1425) In addition, arterial oxygen saturation and blood gas assessment will determine the need for long-term oxygen therapy more accurately at prolonged follow-up compared to shortly after discharge.(1426)
CT assessment to determine the presence of bronchiectasis and emphysema should be done in patients with recurrent exacerbations and/or hospitalizations.(542,1427) A further detailed assessment of the presence and management of comorbidities should also be undertaken (Figure 4.10).(1427)

Prevention of exacerbations
After an acute exacerbation, appropriate measures for prevention of further exacerbations should be initiated (Figure
4.6 and Figure 4.11). For the following treatment modalities significant effects on exacerbation risk/frequency could be shown in clinical trials. For details refer to Chapter 3.
Based on findings from observational studies in various countries(1428-1431) there was a major decrease in hospital admissions for COPD exacerbations during the COVID-19 epidemic. It was hypothesized that this phenomenon may be a consequence of shielding measures (e.g., wearing masks, avoiding social contact, regular hand washing etc).
Accordingly, shielding measures could be considered during the winter months (on top of established pharmacological and non-pharmacological measures) in patients at risk of exacerbation.

CHAPTER 5: COPD AND COMORBIDITIES
KEY POINTS:
• COPD often coexists with other diseases (comorbidities) that may have a significant impact on disease course.
• In general, the presence of comorbidities should not alter COPD treatment and comorbidities should be treated per usual standards regardless of the presence of COPD.
• Cardiovascular diseases are common and important comorbidities in COPD.
• Lung cancer is frequently seen in people with COPD and is a major cause of death. T
B o Annual low-dose CT scan (LDCT) is recommended for lung cancer RscIreening in people with COPD due to smoking according to recommendations fTor the general
S population I
R o Annual LDCT is not recommended for lung cancer screenOing in people with COPD not due to smoking due to insufficient data to establisYh benefit over harm
• Osteoporosis and depression/anxiety are frequent, impoCrtant comorbidities in COPD, are often under-diagnosed, and are associated with poor Thealth status and prognosis.
• Gastroesophageal reflux (GERD) is associated wit h an increased risk of exacerbations and
O poorer health status. D
• People with COPD presenting with newA or worsening respiratory symptoms, fever, and/or
I any other symptoms that could beR COVID-19 related, even if these are mild, should be
E tested for possible infection with SARS-CoV-2 or influenza.
• When COPD is part of a multimorbidity care plan, attention should be directed to ensure
T simplicity of treatmentH and to minimize polypharmacy.
INTRODUCTION
COPD often coexists with other diseases (comorbidities) that may have a significant impact on prognosis.(329,510,511,520,1432-1435) Some of these arise independently of COPD whereas others may be causally related, either with shared risk factors or by one disease increasing the risk or compounding the severity of the other. It is possible that features of COPD, are shared with other diseases and as such this mechanism represents a link between
COPD and some of its comorbidities.(1436,1437) The risk of comorbid disease can be increased by the sequelae of COPD e.g., reduced physical activity or continued smoking. Whether or not COPD and comorbid diseases are related, management of the COPD patient must include identification and treatment of its comorbidities. Importantly, comorbidities with symptoms also associated with COPD may be overlooked e.g., heart failure and lung cancer
(breathlessness) or depression (fatigue and reduced physical activity).
Comorbidities are common at any severity of COPD(182) and the differential diagnosis can often be difficult. For
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 124 example, in a patient with both COPD and heart failure, an exacerbation of COPD may be accompanied by worsening of heart failure or vice versa. Although COPD is negatively impacted by multiple comorbid diseases, COPD itself is one of the most important comorbid conditions that adversely affects the outcomes of other disorders. For example, patients with congestive heart failure or those undergoing cardiac procedures such as coronary artery bypass grafting have greater morbidity and mortality when COPD is present compared to when it is absent.(1438,1439)
Below is a brief guide to the management of some common comorbidities occurring in people with COPD with stable disease. The recommendations may be insufficient for the management of all COPD patients and are not a substitute for the use of guidelines for the management of each individual comorbid condition.
Cardiovascular diseases (CVD)
► A large primary care population study in COPD patients with no history of cardiovascular disease found a 25% increase in the adjusted risk of major adverse cardiac events including acute myocardial infarction, stroke, or cardiovascular death.(1440) E
Heart failure
► The prevalence of systolic or diastolic heart failure in COPD patients ranges froSm 20% to 70%,(1441) and its annual
D incidence is between 3-4%. Incident heart failure is a significant and independent predictor of all-cause mortality.
► Unrecognized heart failure may mimic or accompany acute COPD; Y40% of COPD patients that are mechanically
P ventilated because of hypercapnic respiratory failure have evidenceO of left ventricular dysfunction.(1442,1443)
► Treatment with β -blockers improves survival in heart faOilure and is recommended in patients with heart failure
N who also have COPD. Selective β -blockers should be used, and only used, to treat people with COPD for approved
O cardiovascular indications; not solely for the purpose Dof preventing exacerbations of COPD.(1044)
► Acute heart failure should be treated accoArding to usual heart failure guidelines since there is no evidence to
R support an alternative management strategy. Noninvasive ventilation added to conventional therapy improves
E outcomes for patients with either hyperTcapnic respiratory failure due to an exacerbation of COPD as well as heart
A failure with acute pulmonary edema.M(1444)
Ischaemic heart diseaseG (IHD)
► Ischaemic heart diseYase should be considered in all COPD patients depending on their risk factor profile.
Cardiovascular risk mOay be assessed by the global risk calculator, which can be found on the US National Heart Blood
Lung Institute website(1445) and treatment initiated based on the current recommendations.
► During, and for at least 90 days after, acute COPD exacerbations there is an increased risk of cardiovascular events
(death, myocardial infarction, stroke, unstable angina, and transient ischemic attack) in patients at high risk of concomitant IHD.(1446) Hospitalization for an acute COPD exacerbation has been associated with 90-day mortality of acute myocardial infarction, ischemic stroke, and intracranial hemorrhage.(1447) Patients who demonstrate abnormal cardiac troponins in isolation are at increased risk of adverse outcomes including short-term (30-day) and long-term mortality.(1448,1449)
► The treatment of ischaemic heart disease should be according to guidelines irrespective of the presence of COPD and vice versa.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 125
Arrhythmias
► Cardiac arrhythmias are common in COPD and vice versa.(1450) Atrial fibrillation is frequent and associated with a lower FEV1.(1451) The risk of hospitalization for atrial fibrillation in the first 30 days after an exacerbation of COPD is increased when the exacerbation is severe (hospitalized).(1452)
► In COPD patients presenting with severe worsening dyspnea, associated atrial fibrillation is frequently documented, and it may be either a trigger or a consequence of an acute exacerbation episode.(1453)
► The presence of atrial fibrillation does not alter the treatment of COPD. Bronchodilators have been previously described as potentially pro-arrhythmic agents;(1454,1455) however, available evidence suggests an overall acceptable safety profile for long-acting beta -agonists,(1456) anticholinergic drugs (and ICS).(889,932,968,1457-1461) Nevertheless, caution is advised when using short-acting beta -agonists(1456,1462) and theophylline, which may precipitate atrial fibrillation and make control of the ventricular response rate difficult.(1463-1465)
Pulmonary hypertension U
► Pulmonary hypertension (PH) is defined by an elevated mean pulmonary arterial pressure (mPAP) >20 mmHg, as
S assessed by right heart catheterization.(1466,1467) PH is classified in five distinct groups, based on their different
D pathophysiological mechanisms, clinical presentation and therapeutic manage ment: pulmonary arterial hypertension
(PAH, group 1), PH associated with left heart disease (group 2), PH associateOd with lung diseases and/or hypoxia (group
3), chronic thromboembolic PH (CTEPH, group 4) and PH associated with unclear and/or multifactorial mechanisms
(group 5). All groups of PH can be diagnosed in patients with COPDO.(322) Indeed, COPD is a common disease occurring
C in patients with multiple comorbidities and exposures, which ma y contribute to pulmonary vascular disease.
► Therefore, a diagnosis of PH in a COPD patient require s a careful analysis of the possible mechanisms leading to
O elevated mPAP. This is critical in order to identify treaDtable traits of PH-COPD. For example, CTEPH, a treatable cause
- of PH occurring more frequently in patients above t he age of 40 years, can be detected in patients with COPD.(1468)
► Mild PH is frequent in patients with CEOPD, based on epidemiological estimations 25-30% of all COPD patients
T present with elevated mPAP.(1466,1467,14A69) Most of these patients are classified as “group 3 PH” or “group 2 PH”, if
M significant left heart disease is present. The presence of PH in COPD is clinically relevant, as it is associated with
T increased health-care resource uHtilization, more hospitalizations and a poor prognosis.(324)
► A subgroup of PH-COPYD patients has severe PH, which is of special clinical importance because PH may represent
P a major limiting factoOr in these patients. The 2022 ESC/ERS PH guidelines define severe PH-COPD by an increased PVR
> 5 WU.(1466,1467,1470C) Severe PH-COPD is rare, affecting around 5 % of COPD patients.(1466,1467,1471) The severity of PH is an independent predictor of prognosis(324) and patients with severe PH-COPD frequently present with mild-to- moderate airflow limitation, no or very mild hypercapnia, a low DLco <45% predicted and a circulatory exercise limitation. For this phenotype of COPD patients the term “pulmonary vascular phenotype” has been suggested.(322)
► Echocardiography is the best non-invasive tool to estimate the probability and severity of PH.(324,1466,1467) In addition, elevated NT-proBNP and increased ratio of the diameter of the pulmonary artery and the aorta (PA:Ao ratio) on HR CT are associated with the presence of PH-COPD. Of note, the diameter of the pulmonary artery has also been associated with the risk of COPD exacerbations.(325)
► Patients with PH-COPD should be referred to a PH center with experience in respiratory diseases where they will undergo right heart catheterization and multidisciplinary assessment to guide treatment decision (Figure 5.1). Long- term oxygen therapy is recommended in hypoxemic patients.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 126
► When PAH is diagnosed in a patient with COPD, management shOould follow the 2022 ESC/ERS Guidelines. In such
C patients, the preferred option is initial monotherapy followed by careful sequential combination therapy if the
T treatment goals are not reached. O
► When CTEPH is diagnosed in a patient with COPD, Dmultidisciplinary CTEPH team assessment will consider whether pulmonary endarterectomy, angioplasty and/or PH -medications are indicated.
► In patients with non-severe PH-COPD the use of PAH medications Is not recommended.
► In patients with severe PH-COPD Man individualized approach is suggested.(1466,1467) In clinical practice, off-label use
T of phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors has been most frequently proposed to treat these patients.(1472,1473) However,
H the results of the PERFECT triaGl highlight the challenges of the management of PH-COPD. In this study, treatment with
R inhaled treprostinil showed no clinical efficacy, but was associated with potential adverse events. Further well Y designed randomized-cPontrolled trials should be encouraged to firmly advise for or against the use of PAH medications
O in PH-COPD.(1474) C
Peripheral vascular disease
► Peripheral artery disease (PAD) is commonly associated with atherosclerotic heart disease and may have significant implications for functional activity as well as quality of life in people with COPD.(1475)
► In a large cohort of people with COPD of all degrees of severity, 8.8% were diagnosed with PAD that was higher than the prevalence in non-COPD controls (1.8%).(1475)
► COPD patients with PAD reported a worse functional capacity and worse health status compared to those without
PAD. Clinicians should consider PAD in people with COPD to those at risk for vascular events and to fully understand their functional impairments.
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 127
Hypertension
► Hypertension is likely to be the most frequently occurring comorbidity in COPD and may have implications for prognosis.(1436,1437) Diastolic dysfunction as a result of sub-optimally treated hypertension may be associated with exercise intolerance and mimic symptoms associated with an acute exacerbation thereby provoking hospitalization in
COPD.(1441) These data stress the importance of optimal blood pressure control in COPD patients with underlying hypertension.(1476,1477)
► Hypertension should be treated according to usual guidelines. There is no evidence that hypertension should be treated differently in the presence of COPD. The role of treatment with selective beta-blockers is less prominent in recent hypertension guidelines and there is no evidence that in people with COPD and increased cardiovascular risk cardio-selective beta-blockers either reduce the benefits of treatment with LABA or increase cardiovascular risk.(1478)
► COPD should be treated as usual as there is no direct evidence that COPD should be treated differently in the
E presence of hypertension. T
Lung cancer I
Lung cancer is the leading cause of death from malignant disease worldwide, with more deaths from lung cancer than
D from colon, breast and prostate cancer together and it causes an estimate d 1.6 million deaths worldwide each
R year.(1479) Unfortunately, the great majority of lung cancers are diagnoseOd at an advanced stage, resulting in poor
Y overall survival.(1480) Therefore, primary, and secondary prevention and early detection are important to improve
P survival. There is evidence for an association between COPD and luOng cancer that has been systematically confirmed
C in several epidemiological and observational cohort studies.(1437,14 81-1483) These two diseases appear to share more than
T tobacco exposure as their common origin. Genetic suscepOtibility, epigenetic changes in DNA methylation, local
N pulmonary chronic inflammation and abnormal lung repa ir mechanisms present in COPD are also thought to be the
O most important potential contributors to lung cancDer development.(1484-1486) Whether the spirometric severity of
- airflow obstruction is directly or inversely associ ated with a greater risk for lung cancer development remains
A controversial.(416,1483) The association between lung cancer and degree of emphysema is stronger than that existing
R between lung cancer and degree of airfElow obstruction and the greatest risk is observed in people with the
T combination of emphysema diagnosedA by CT and airflow obstruction determined by spirometry.(1487,1488) The best
M preventive measure for lung cancer (as it is for COPD) is smoking prevention and in smokers, smoking cessation.(1489)
Several studies involving the use of low-dose chest computed tomography (LDCT) screening have shown improved
R survival.(442,443,1490) The UnYited States Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) updated its recommendation for lung
P cancer screening in 2O021.(1491) Their recommendation was based on a systematic review that examined the accuracy of screening for lunCg cancer considering the benefits and harms associated with lung cancer screening. USPSTF also commissioned collaborative modeling studies from the National Cancer Institute (NCI) Cancer Intervention and surveillance modeling Network (CISNET) to provide the optimal age to begin and end lung cancer screening, the optimal screening interval, and to assess the relative benefits and harms of different screening strategies. The UPSTF now recommends annual screening for lung cancer with LDCT in adults aged 50-80 years who have a 20-pack year smoking history and currently smoke or quit smoking within the past 15 years. They recommend stopping screening once a person has not smoked for 15 years or develops a health problem that substantially limits life expectancy or the ability or willingness to have curative lung surgery. Additionally, the CISNET modeling analysis supports screening at a younger age with a lower smoking burden to address current racial and gender disparities that exist with lung cancer screening.(1491-1495) In patients with smoking related COPD, annual screening for lung cancer with LDCT should be conducted in those 50-80 years of age with a 20-pack year smoking history who currently smoke, or who have quit smoking within the past 15 years. COPD has also been reported to be an independent risk factor for lung cancer incidence in never smokers.(1496,1497) Risk factors include biomass fuel exposure, second-hand smoke, radon, air
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 128 pollution, a family history of lung cancer, and asbestos exposure. Routine annual screening with LDCT has not been conducted in people with COPD who are never smokers and annual LDCT screening is not currently recommended because the possible harms of screening seem to outweigh the possible benefit of finding early lung cancer.(1498)
Although this recommendation is supported by several major medical societies several important questions remain.
Several studies have suggested that the yield of CT screening would improve if additional variables such as age, smoking history, BMI, presence of airflow obstruction and or emphysema and family history of lung cancer were added to the current screening criteria.(446,1499)
The implementation of a screening program, where available, could be useful, but has to be implemented in the appropriate environment to avoid over diagnosis, greater morbidity and mortality with needless diagnostic procedures for benign abnormalities, anxiety and incomplete follow-up, as has been suggested by studies in primary care.(1500) On the other hand, one Danish study showed that being part of a lung cancer screening programme significantly promotes smoking abstinence(1501) and a review of different studies concluded that smoking cessation during LDCT screening
E results in improved spirometry as well as a decrease in micronodules seen on the baselTine CT, thus beneficially
U affecting lung cancer and COPD.(1489) Smoking cessation interventions as part of CT scan sBcreening programs could be
R of use (Figure 5.2).
Inhaled corticosCteroids (ICS) and lung cancer incidence
ICS are recommended in selected people with COPD and their potential impact on development of lung cancer has been the subject of conflicting reports. Several retrospective analyses of large databases or observational cohorts(1502) have suggested a reduction in lung cancer risk with the use of ICS but confounding factors have not been consistently controlled for in all studies.(1503-1508) A more pronounced protective effect of ICS was reported in former compared to current smokers,(1506) those with a concurrent diagnosis of asthma(1508) or, those prescribed a higher dose of ICS.(1507)
A systematic review that included two observational studies and 4 RCTs, reported a protective effect of ICS on lung cancer risk in the observational studies that used a higher dose of ICS, but no benefit in the RCTs.(1509) An analysis designed to avoid immortal time bias (985) and an observational study (> 65,000 patients) reported no effect of ICS use on lung cancer incidence.(1510) In contrast, one database study reported an increased risk of lung cancer in patients prescribed ICS compared to those not prescribed ICS.(1511) Reports from large prospective RCTs focused on lung function decline, exacerbation reduction or mortality, conducted in patients with moderate to severe COPD where cause of death was analyzed using clinical end-point committees reported no difference in cancer deaths in patients randomized to ICS versus non-ICS use.(496,699,844,889,968,1460)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 129
The conflicting results between observational and RCTs are probably due to differences in the patient populations, characterization of lung cancer risk, follow-up time (shorter in interventional trials), impact of immortal time bias, and the rigorousness used to detect lung cancer. Based on the available data ICS do not appear to increase or decrease the risk of lung cancer pending studies adequately planned to clarify these important questions.
COVID-19 and COPD
► People with COPD presenting with new or worsening respiratory symptoms, fever, and/or any other symptoms that could be COVID-19 related, even if these are mild, should be tested for possible infection with SARS-CoV-2.
► Patients should keep taking their oral and inhaled respiratory medications for COPD as directed.
► Physical distancing and shielding, or sheltering-in-place, should not lead to social isolation and inactivity. Patients should stay in contact with their friends and families by telecommunication and continue to keEep active. They should
T also ensure they have enough medication. U
► Patients should be encouraged to use reputable resources for medical informaTtion regarding COVID-19 and its
S management. D I
► Guidance for remote (phone/virtual/online) COPD patient follow-up an d a printable checklist are provided.
► Differentiating the symptoms of COVID-19 infection from the uCsual symptoms of COPD can be challenging. Cough and breathlessness are found in over 60% of patients with COVITD-19 but are usually also accompanied by fever (> 60%
O of patients) as well as fatigue, confusion, diarrhea, nausea,N vomiting, muscle aches and pains, anosmia, dysgeusia and headaches.(1512) O
 ► In COVID-19 symptoms may be mild at first, bLut rapid deterioration in lung function may occur. The prodrome of
A milder symptoms is especially problematic inR pIatients with underlying COPD who may already have diminished lung
E reserve. Lack of recognition of the prodromal symptoms may delay early diagnosis and preliminary data suggest that
A people with COPD reporting exacerbations and suspected of having COVID-19 infection were infrequently tested for
M its presence.(1513) A high index of su spicion for COVID-19 needs to be maintained in people with COPD who present
H with symptoms of an exacerbation, especially if accompanied by fever, impaired taste or smell or GI complaints.
► Persistent symptoms in people with COPD may cause diagnostic difficulty. Approximately 10-20% of patients
P infected with SARS-COoV-2 may develop persistent symptoms with 25-45% experiencing dyspnea at 6-9 months after
C the acute infection and approximately 20% at 9-12 months.(1514-1517) Delayed recovery was more common in people with multiple chronic medical conditions including COPD.(1518-1520)
Bronchiectasis
► With increasing use of computed tomography in the assessment of people with COPD, the presence of previously unrecognized bronchiectasis is being identified.(1521) The prevalence of bronchiectasis in COPD patients has been analyzed in several studies with conflicting results ranging from 20% to 69% (mean prevalence was 54.3%).(349,1522)
► Whether this diagnosis based on radiological criteria has the same impact as a clinical diagnosis of bronchiectasis remains unknown at present. Two systematic reviews and meta-analyses have compared the characteristics of COPD patients with and without bronchiectasis. The results indicated that people with COPD and comorbid bronchiectasis are more often male with a longer smoking history, greater daily sputum production, more frequent exacerbations, poorer lung function, higher level of inflammatory biomarkers, more chronic colonization by potentially pathogenic
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 130 microorganisms, higher rate of Pseudomonas aeruginosa isolation and increased mortality.(348,349)
► Bronchiectasis should be treated according to usual guidelines.
► Regarding COPD treatment, some patients may need more aggressive and prolonged antibiotic therapy. ICS may not be indicated in patients with bacterial colonization or recurrent lower respiratory tract infections.
Obstructive sleep apnea & insomnia
► COPD has an estimated prevalence in U.S. adults of 13.9%(1523,1524) and obstructive sleep apnea (OSA), a sleep disorder hallmarked by repeated episodes of upper airway closure, affects 9% to 26% of the U.S. adult population.(1525)
► Patients with both COPD and OSA have a worse prognosis compared with either condition alone.(1526) During sleep, patients with both COPD and OSA suffer more frequent episodes of oxygen desaturation and have more total sleep time with hypoxemia and hypercapnia than OSA patients without COPD.(1527) E
► The apneic events in patients with combined OSA and COPD have more profound Ihypoxemia and more cardiac
R arrhythmias.(1528) Additionally, patients with combined COPD and OSA are more likeTly to develop daytime pulmonary
S hypertension(1529,1530) than patients with just OSA or COPD alone. D I
► The use of positive pressure ventilation in patients with COPD and O SA has been reported to reduce all-cause
Y hospitalizations, emergency room visits, moderate and severe exacerbPations and associated healthcare costs.(781,1531)
► Insomnia in COPD is associated with higher rates of outpatieTnts’ visits and hospitalizations.(1532)
Periodontitis & dental hygiene O
The association between COPD and periodontitis ha-s been noted mainly in the dental literature although whether this
L reflects common causative factors such as agAe, smoking and socioeconomic circumstances remains speculative.
Although both conditions have a common (neutrophilic) relationship whether this reflects cause or effect is difficult
E to elucidate.(1533) In a more complete stuTdy the data supported shared pathophysiology between periodontitis and
COPD with similar aberrant neutrophMil function, especially when associated with alpha-1-antitrypsin deficiency.(1534)
The risk of developing periodoGntitis increases with the number of emergency room visits for COPD.(1535) High antibody
R levels to common periodontal pathogens is associated with less exacerbations of COPD.(1536) In a recent systematic
Y review low to moderateP evidence suggests that periodontal treatment is associated with slower lung function decline,
O reduced frequencyC of exacerbations and less use of healthcare resources in patients with COPD and chronic periodontitis.(1537) In the absence of an effective curative treatment for COPD it is difficult to prove the reverse is also true.
Nevertheless, periodontitis is common in COPD and often requires treatment in its own right which may lead to a reduction in exacerbations.
Metabolic syndrome and diabetes
► Studies have shown that metabolic syndrome and manifest diabetes are more frequent in COPD and likely to affect prognosis.(510,1538)
► Insulin resistance has been associated with increased risk of COPD in women but not in men.(1539)
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 131
► The prevalence of metabolic syndrome has been estimated to be more than 30%.(1540)
► Diabetes should be treated according to usual guidelines for diabetes. COPD should be treated as usual.
Gastroesophageal reflux (GERD)
► GERD is an independent risk factor for exacerbations and is associated with worse health status.(487,1541,1542) The mechanisms responsible for increased risk of exacerbations are not yet fully established.
► Proton pump inhibitors are often used for treatment of GERD. One small, single-blind study suggested these agents decrease the risk of exacerbation,(1543) but their value in preventing these events remains controversial most effective treatment for this condition in COPD has yet to be established.(1544,1545)
Osteoporosis
► Osteoporosis is an important and common comorbidity(511,1436)which is often under-diagnTosed(1546)and associated
U with poor health status and prognosis. B
► Osteoporosis is often associated with emphysema,(1547) decreased body mass inSdex(1548) and low fat-free mass.(1549)
Low bone mineral density and fractures are commonly in COPD patients even after adjustment for steroid use, age,
R pack-years of smoking, current smoking, and exacerbations.(1550,1551) O
► Osteoporosis should be treated according to usual guidelines. O
► COPD should be treated as usual despite the presence ofO osteoporosis. An association between ICS and fractures
N has been found in pharmaco- epidemiological studies; however, these studies have not fully taken severity of COPD
O or exacerbations and their treatment into account. D
► Systemic corticosteroids significantly incrAease the risk of osteoporosis and repeated courses for COPD
R exacerbations should be avoided if possible
Anemia M
Anemia is frequent in people wHith COPD, with a reported prevalence of 7.5% to 34%.(1552) People with COPD and
G anemia are generally older, hIave more frequent cardiometabolic comorbidities, greater dyspnea, worse quality of life
R and airflow obstruction, rYeduced exercise capacity, an increased risk of severe exacerbations and higher mortality.(1552 P
1558) O
Anemia due to chronic disease is the most common type seen in COPD, followed by iron deficiency anemia,(1559,1560) and is mainly related to chronic systemic inflammation and impaired iron utilization. However, other possible reversible factors should be investigated including use of long-term oxygen, theophylline, angiotensin-converting- enzyme inhibitors, angiotensin II receptor inhibitors, renal dysfunction, and androgens.(1561-1569)
Although anemia has been established as an important comorbidity in COPD, optimal hemoglobin and hematocrit levels in these patients have not yet been defined, and it is also unclear whether its correction alters outcomes.
However, hemoglobin assessment is advisable, particularly in more severely affected patients. If anemia is diagnosed, a systematic search for a treatable cause is recommended in accordance with appropriate clinical guidelines.
Polycythemia
Secondary polycythemia has long been recognized as a common comorbidity in COPD with a reported prevalence of
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 132
6% to 10.2% in COPD outpatients (when defined as hemoglobin ≥ 17g/dL in males and ≥ 15g/dL in females).(1554,1556,1570)
Interestingly, in the COPDGene cohorts 9.2% of men and 3.5% of women had secondary polycythemia.(1571) Although the prevalence of polycythemia in COPD has decreased following the introduction of long-term oxygen therapy
(LTOT),(1572) one study reported a prevalence of 8.4% in patients with severe COPD receiving LTOT.(1555)
Data from a large cohort (COPDGene cohort) of individuals with moderate to very severe COPD, indicate that male sex, current smoking, living at high altitude (e.g., Denver, Colorado, USA), impaired DLco, and severe hypoxemia were associated with increased risk for secondary polycythemia, whereas, LTOT use was associated with a decreased risk of polycythemia.(1571) The coexistence of obstructive sleep apnea has also been associated with increased risk of polycythemia in patients with COPD.(1573) Smoking causes an increase in carboxyhemoglobin, thereby increasing red blood cell mass and risk of secondary polycythemia in people with COPD.(1574,1575)
Secondary polycythemia in COPD may be associated with pulmonary hypertension,(1576,1577) venous thromboembolism,(1577) and mortality.(1578,1579) However, these findings should be interpreted with caution, since
E secondary polycythemia may be related to the presence of severe uncorrected hypoxemiaT, which is a predictor of
U mortality in COPD, as well as to the presence of concomitant interstitial lung disease or pBulmonary vascular disease.
In COPD, if secondary polycythemia is present, a careful evaluation should be performed to determine uncorrected
D hypoxemia or to rule out the presence of any comorbidities that require a spec ific intervention.
Anxiety and depression Y
► Anxiety and depression are important and underdiagnosedC comorbidities in COPD(361,1580-1582) and both are associated with a poor prognosis,(1581,1583) younger age, femaleT sex, smoking, lower FEV1, cough, higher SGRQ score,
O and a history of cardiovascular disease.(43,361,1582) N
► There is no evidence that anxiety and depression should be treated differently in the presence of COPD.
► COPD should be treated as usual in patRieInts with psychological disorders. The potential impact of pulmonary
E rehabilitation should be stressed as studies have found that physical exercise has a beneficial effect on depression in
A general.(1584,1585)
► COPD is very common in patients with other psychiatric illnesses, often under-diagnosed and treated.(1586,1587)
► A systematic review has shown that COPD patients are 1.9 times more likely to commit suicide than people without
COPD.(1588) O
► Following a diagnosis of COPD patients are more likely to develop depression and the risk is greater in patients with worse breathlessness.(1589)
Cognitive impairment
► Cognitive impairment (CI) is common in people with COPD.(1590) An average prevalence of 32% has been suggested.(1591) The prevalence and severity varies by the type of assessment.(1592) Extensive neuropsychological testing suggests that up to 56% of patients may suffer CI.(1593,1594) Longitudinal studies suggest greater risk of developing CI in
COPD diagnosed in midlife,(1590,1595) and associate COPD with the development of dementia.(1596)
► CI has been reported in patients across the entire range of spirometric severity.(1594)
► CI has been associated with impairment in basic activities of daily living,(1597,1598) and variably associated with
Be sure to read and understand the paragraph entitled Important Purpose & Liability Disclaimer 133 impaired health status.(1599,1600)
► The coexistence of CI and COPD has been associated with an increased risk of hospitalization(1601) and increased length of stay during acute exacerbation hospitalization.(1602)
► The impact of CI on self-management skills in COPD patients remains unclear,(1597) although inhaler incompetency has been linked to CI.(1597)
Frailty
► Frailty can be defined as the presence of five components: weakness, slowness, exhaustion, low physical activity, and unintentional weight loss.(1603)
► In a cohort study, the prevalence of frailty among individuals with COPD was higher than in individuals without
COPD and may help identify people with COPD at risk of poor outcomes.(1604) E
► In a meta-analysis it was reported that frailty and pre-frailty were associated wIith all-cause mortality, acute
R exacerbation, and hospitalization in patients with COPD.(1605) T
► The European Respiratory Society has published a review of current evRidence on treating frailty in adults with
O chronic respiratory disease and includes clinical management options suc h as geriatric care, rehabilitation, nutrition,
Y pharmacological and psychological therapies.(1606) P
COPD as part of multimorbidity T
► An increasing number of people in any aging population will suffer from multi-morbidity, defined as the presence
O of two or more chronic conditions, and COPD is preseDnt in most multi-morbid patients.
► Multi-morbid patients have symptoms from Amultiple diseases and thus symptoms and signs are complex and most
R often attributable to several causes in the chronic state as well as during acute events.
► There is no evidence that COPD shMould be treated differently when part of multi-morbidity; however, it should be kept in mind that most evidence co T mes from trials in people with COPD as the only significant disease.(512)
► Treatments should be kept simple in the light of the unbearable polypharmacy that these patients are often
Y exposed to. P
Other considerations
► Consider checking for vitamin D deficiency in COPD patients.

Instructions for using the COPD follow-up checklist
1. Introduction a. Identify dates, Dx and whether this follow-up is being done in-person, by phone or remotely.
2. Section 1 – Baseline symptoms a. Go over the patient symptoms and whether there have been changes in dyspnea, cough, sputum volume and color (from least to most purulent: mucus; mucopurulent; purulent).
b. Identify maintenance pharmacological and non-pharmacological treatment and whether the patient is observing treatment as prescribed.
3. Section 2 – COVID-19 a. Assess whether the patient has any symptoms of COVID-19 and would need to be tested. Have at hand local numbers where the patient can be referred to for testing and treatment.
b. If the patient has already been tested identify when the results will be obtained, or whether the result was positive or negative. If positive, is there a follow-up test planned, and dates.
E c. Verify patient is practicing COVID-19 precautions (face masks, hand washingT, social distancing, or
U shielding if necessary).
4. Section 3 – Action Plan R I
T a. Describe if the patient already has a written action plan. See examSple of an action plan from the
Living well with COPD program.(801) Describe if the education foDr this action plan has already been
R done. Describe if the written action plan includes a prescription to be self-administered at home or
O whether the patient need to call his contact person / phy sician to obtain the prescription. Describe
P when it was used the last time and if used appropriately.
5. Section 4 – Recent Admissions and ER visits C
T a. Write down recent admissions and ER visits, dates and where they took place.
6. Section 5 – COPD Self-Management behaviors N
O a. Go over each of the self-management behaviors described in the list. You should cover what is
D pertinent to the patient treatable tr-a its (dyspnea and/or exacerbation). Describe whether the
L patient has integrated these strAategies in their daily life (yes), not at all (e.g., it has not been
I discussed or not applicable),R and whether the patient is unsure “cannot tell”.
7. Section 6 – Main issues T
A a. Identify with the patMient the main issues of the call. Up to a maximum of 3 items that can be covered for the duration ofT the call. Avoid covering too many issues in one visit.
8. Section 7 – Summary,G Intervention and Plan
I a. Finalize by Rdescribing the interventions done during the remote visit, the ones to be put in place, and
Y agreed Pby the patient, the plan, including whether the patient needs to be referred to other services,
O healthcare professionals, etc. and when the next follow-up will take place (describe whether will it
C be in-person or remote).
